Brown treats this as a genuinely notable authorial choice: either John assumes his audience already knows the Bethlehem tradition and needs no reminder, or he is simply uninterested in resolving an objection his Gospel's larger argument, belief based on Jesus's works and identity rather than his birth record, does not require him to settle.

107 — The prophecy behind the objection

Micah 5:2 (RSV): 'But you, O Bethlehem Ephrathah, who are too little to be among the clans of Judah, from you shall come forth for me one who is to be ruler in Israel, whose origin is from of old, from ancient days.' This eighth-century BCE oracle is the specific scriptural basis the Tabernacles crowd invokes in John 7:42 when objecting that the Christ cannot come from Galilee; the passage names Bethlehem as a place of origin, while John's Jesus is known publicly only as a Galilean.

108 — Drawing water from Siloam: the ceremony behind living water

Mishnah, Sukkah 4:9-10 (trans. Herbert Danby, The Mishnah, Oxford: Oxford University Press, 1933), describing the water-libation ceremony (Hebrew: nisuch ha-mayim) performed each morning of the Feast of Tabernacles: a priest processed to the Pool of Siloam, filled a golden flask, and carried it back to the Temple to be poured out at the base of the altar alongside the wine libation, accompanied by flute music and celebration so joyful the Mishnah elsewhere calls it the model for all rejoicing. Mainstream commentary, see Raymond E. Brown, Anchor Bible vol. 29, and Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John, Volume 1, both ad loc. John 7:37-39, reads Jesus's cry that whoever thirsts should come to him and drink as a direct, concrete response to this ongoing ritual rather than a free-floating metaphor detached from its festival setting.

109 — Which day was the great day?

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 7:37. Commentators are not fully agreed on which day John means by the last day, the great day of the feast: the seventh day, later called Hoshana Rabbah in rabbinic tradition, when the water-libation and willow-branch processions reached their most elaborate form, is the more commonly proposed candidate, though some argue for the added eighth day (Shemini Atzeret), a solemn assembly on which the water rite was not performed at all. This is a live ambiguity in reconstructing the festival calendar rather than a settled point.

110 — Whose heart? A punctuation crux, not a copying error

Nestle-Aland, Novum Testamentum Graece, 28th ed., apparatus and paragraphing ad loc. John 7:37-38; discussed at length in Raymond E. Brown, Anchor Bible vol. 29, and C. K. Barrett, The Gospel According to St John, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1978), both ad loc. Ancient Greek manuscripts carried no punctuation, and John 7:37-38 can be divided two different ways: the traditional reading makes the believer's own heart the source of the rivers of living water, while an equally grammatical alternative places the sentence break after let him drink, making Jesus's own body the source instead. This is a genuine, still-debated interpretive crux rather than a manuscript variant in the ordinary sense, and it changes what the verse is actually claiming.

111 — Who were the officers?

E. P. Sanders, Judaism: Practice and Belief, 63 BCE-66 CE (London: SCM Press; Philadelphia: Trinity Press International, 1992), discussion of Temple administration and personnel. The officers (Greek hyperetai) whom the chief priests and Pharisees dispatch to arrest Jesus (7:32, 45) were very likely members of the Temple's own Levitical guard, a police force under high-priestly authority responsible for order in the Temple precincts, not Roman soldiers and not acting on any Roman legal authority. That distinction matters for how the scene's failed arrest and Nicodemus's due-process objection read against actual first-century Jewish legal procedure.

112 — Truth spoken by the wrong mouth

Rudolf Bultmann, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, trans. G. R. Beasley-Murray (Oxford: Basil Blackwell, 1971), commentary ad loc. John 7:45-46. Bultmann's classic treatment of Johannine style identifies a recurring pattern in which a minor or hostile character speaks a line that is truer than the character himself realizes, here the arresting officers returning empty-handed with the report that no one ever spoke like this man, a device Bultmann reads as central to the Gospel's ironic, layered narration rather than as incidental color.

113 — Nicodemus, in three acts

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday, 1966), introductory discussion of Nicodemus; D. Moody Smith, John, Abingdon New Testament Commentaries (Nashville: Abingdon Press, 1999), commentary ad loc. 7:50-51. Both commentators note that Nicodemus appears at exactly three points in John's narrative, as a cautious night-time questioner (3:1-21), as a lone, procedurally framed defender here (7:50-52), and finally as an open participant in Jesus's burial (19:39-40), and that the three scenes read as a deliberately staged, incremental arc rather than three unconnected cameos, moving him from private curiosity toward public, if still restrained, association with Jesus.

114 — A prophet did arise from Galilee

D. A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 7:52. The Pharisees' retort to Nicodemus, that no prophet arises from Galilee, does not hold up against their own scriptures: the eighth-century BCE prophet Jonah is explicitly identified in 2 Kings 14:25 as coming from Gath-hepher, a town in Galilee. Whether John intends readers to catch this as a pointed irony against the Pharisees' scriptural sloppiness, or whether it is simply an overstated debating point of the kind people make in an argument, the objection itself is demonstrably false as scripture.

115 — A live theological question, not a rhetorical throwaway

Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, 2 vols. (Peabody, MA: Hendrickson Publishers, 2003), commentary ad loc. John 9:2. Keener documents that the disciples' question, whether the man's own sin or his parents' caused his congenital blindness, reflects a genuinely live theological option in Second Temple and early rabbinic Judaism rather than a naive or invented framing: alongside the standard reading of inherited guilt (a parent's sin visited on a child), later rabbinic tradition also speculated, building on texts like Genesis 25:22 (Jacob and Esau struggling within Rebekah's womb), that a fetus could itself act, and even sin, before birth — making "this man himself," despite being unborn at the time of any wrongdoing, a coherent candidate for blame within that framework.

116 — Two competing scripts for suffering

D. A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids, MI: Wm. B. Eerdmans, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 9:2-3. Carson situates the disciples' dilemma against a real tension within Israel's own scriptures: Deuteronomy 5:9 and Exodus 20:5 describe God "visiting the iniquity of the fathers upon the children," while Ezekiel 18:20 insists just as forcefully that "the son shall not suffer for the iniquity of the father" and that each person answers for their own sin alone. Second Temple Judaism had not resolved that tension into a single settled doctrine, which is precisely why the disciples can pose their either/or question as a genuine, open puzzle rather than a rhetorical setup.

117 — An answer that declines the premise

D. A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids, MI: Wm. B. Eerdmans, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 9:3. Carson reads Jesus's reply, "it was not that this man sinned, or his parents, but that the works of God might be displayed in him," as declining to adjudicate the disciples' retributive either/or at all, substituting a teleological purpose for a causal diagnosis. Mainstream commentary treats this as consistent with the wider Gospel tradition's suspicion of facile suffering-equals-punishment reasoning, comparable to Jesus's comment elsewhere about the Galileans killed by Pilate and those crushed by the falling tower in Siloam (Luke 13:1-5), where the same refusal to read catastrophe as targeted divine punishment recurs.

118 — Spittle as recognized medicine in the ancient world

Pliny the Elder, Natural History, Book 28 (Loeb Classical Library, trans. W. H. S. Jones, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 1963), sections on the medicinal properties of human saliva. Pliny catalogues a range of therapeutic and apotropaic uses ancient Mediterranean medicine attributed to human spittle, including its application to the eyes, reflecting a widely shared folk-medical framework in which saliva was not a strange or arbitrary healing agent but a recognized, commonly invoked remedy across the Greco-Roman world.

119 — The same Vespasian tradition, a familiar comparandum

Cornelius Tacitus, Histories, Book 4, chapter 81 (Loeb Classical Library, trans. Clifford H. Moore, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 1931); Suetonius, Lives of the Caesars: Vespasian, section 7 (Loeb Classical Library, trans. J. C. Rolfe, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 1914). Both historians record, independently of each other and of any Christian source, that the emperor Vespasian was petitioned at Alexandria by a blind man asking that Vespasian moisten his eyes with spittle, and that the man reportedly recovered his sight; Suetonius adds a lame man healed by touch in the same episode. This is the same widely cited Vespasian-saliva tradition already noted as a comparandum for Gospel spittle-healings elsewhere in this project's treatment of the Synoptic tradition; it remains here what it was there — independent evidence that healing by spittle was a recognizable category of claim in this period, not proof of any specific healing's mechanism.

120 — A name the Gospel stops to translate

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 9:7. John pauses his narrative to gloss the pool's Hebrew-derived name for a Greek-speaking audience: "Siloam (which means Sent)." Brown reads the gloss as deliberate theological wordplay rather than incidental translation, since "sent" (apostellein) is one of the Fourth Gospel's own recurring descriptions of Jesus's relationship to the Father who sent him; the man is, in effect, sent to the pool called Sent by the one whom the Gospel repeatedly calls the Sent One.

121 — The excavated pool behind the story

Hershel Shanks, "The Siloam Pool: Where Jesus Cured the Blind Man," Biblical Archaeology Review 31, no. 5 (September/October 2005). Building on the Second Temple-period stepped pool already established in this project's discussion of John 7, Shanks reports on archaeologists Ronny Reich and Eli Shukron's 2004 discovery of the actual pool complex, confirming a real, physically excavated first-century site of the kind this chapter's narrative sends the man born blind to wash in — a genuine archaeological anchor beneath this specific pericope, not merely the earlier one.

122 — Why making mud counted as work

Mishnah, Shabbat 7:2 (trans. Herbert Danby, The Mishnah, Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1933). The Mishnah's list of the thirty-nine primary categories of labor forbidden on the Sabbath includes kneading (lash) among its enumerated forms of prohibited work. Mainstream commentary reads the Pharisees' objection in this chapter as resting on exactly this kind of category: mixing spittle and dust into mud (9:6) was itself a recognizable act of "kneading," making Jesus's healing method a plausible Sabbath violation in the eyes of his strictest critics, independent of anything about the healing's result.

123 — The one exception that swallows the rule

Mishnah, Yoma 8:6 (trans. Herbert Danby, The Mishnah, Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1933). The Mishnah states that any doubt about danger to life overrides the Sabbath — the classic rabbinic principle, later termed pikuach nefesh, permitting Sabbath law to be set aside to save a life. A chronic, non-life-threatening condition like lifelong blindness falls outside that exception under later rabbinic reasoning, which is precisely why the healing in this chapter could plausibly register to some observers as unnecessary, unforced labor rather than a justified emergency intervention.

124 — A word that appears nowhere else in the New Testament

Raymond E. Brown, An Introduction to the Gospel of John, ed. Francis J. Moloney (New York: Doubleday, 2003), discussion of the Johannine community's history and its conflict with the synagogue. Brown notes that aposynagogos, the term behind "put out of the synagogue" at 9:22, recurs only twice more in the entire New Testament, both later in this same Gospel (12:42; 16:2), and nowhere in the Synoptics, Paul, or Acts. That narrow, exclusively Johannine concentration is itself a data point mainstream scholarship weighs heavily: whatever historical experience stands behind the word, it appears to be this Gospel's own distinctive concern rather than a widely shared feature of the broader early Christian movement's memory.

125 — A drama read on two levels at once

J. Louis Martyn, History and Theology in the Fourth Gospel, 3rd ed. (Louisville, KY: Westminster John Knox Press, 2003; 1st ed. 1968), using John 9 as the book's central case study. Martyn argues the chapter's aposynagogos detail is anachronistic as a feature of Jesus's own actual ministry setting — there is no independent evidence of an operative, formal synagogue policy specifically expelling confessors of Jesus as Messiah in the 20s-30s CE — and instead reflects the Johannine community's own later, historically real experience of exclusion from synagogue life, retrojected into the narrative present of Jesus's ministry. On Martyn's reading, the healed man's own escalating confrontation with the authorities doubles as a portrait of the Johannine community's own confrontation with the synagogue of its own day.

126 — Reconstructing a community from its own Gospel

Raymond E. Brown, The Community of the Beloved Disciple (New York: Paulist Press, 1979), chapter on the Johannine community's history and its break with the synagogue. Brown draws directly on this chapter's expulsion scene, among other Johannine passages, to reconstruct a plausible history of the Gospel's own originating community: a Jewish-Christian group whose confession of Jesus's identity precipitated a real, painful separation from the wider synagogue community that had been their own religious home, an experience Brown argues left a deep imprint on how the Gospel itself narrates conflict and rejection.

127 — A redactional layer with its own historical moment

Urban C. von Wahlde, The Gospel and Letters of John, vol. 2: Commentary on the Gospel of John, Eerdmans Critical Commentary (Grand Rapids, MI: Wm. B. Eerdmans, 2010), introduction and commentary ad loc. John 9. Von Wahlde's source- and redaction-critical analysis of the Gospel's compositional layers associates the aposynagogos passages, including this chapter's, with what he identifies as a later editorial stage of the Gospel's growth, a stage he argues responds directly to an intensifying, historically datable conflict between the Johannine community and the synagogue — an independent redaction-critical line of argument that converges with Martyn's and Brown's community-history readings without depending on either.

128 — The Twelfth Benediction, and the question of its date

J. Louis Martyn, History and Theology in the Fourth Gospel, 3rd ed. (Louisville, KY: Westminster John Knox Press, 2003), discussion of the Birkat ha-Minim. Rabbinic tradition credits Samuel the Small, at the academy of Yavneh under the patriarch Gamaliel II in the closing decades of the first century CE, with composing or revising the Twelfth Benediction of the daily Amidah prayer to include a curse against minim (sectarians or heretics), a formula Martyn's original thesis proposed as the specific mechanism generating John's aposynagogos passages — a benediction whose recitation a confessing Jewish Christian could not honestly complete.

129 — A tighter link than the evidence supports

Reuven Kimelman, "Birkat Ha-Minim and the Lack of Evidence for an Anti-Christian Jewish Prayer in Late Antiquity," in Jewish and Christian Self-Definition, vol. 2, ed. E. P. Sanders, A. I. Baumgarten, and Alan Mendelson (Philadelphia: Fortress Press, 1981); Steven T. Katz, "Issues in the Separation of Judaism and Christianity after 70 C.E.: A Reconsideration," Journal of Biblical Literature 103, no. 1 (1984): 43-76. Both Kimelman and Katz independently challenge Martyn's original tight linkage of John's expulsion passages to the Birkat ha-Minim specifically, questioning the benediction's precise date, its original target, and whether any synagogue in this period possessed the centralized authority to enforce a uniform exclusion policy against Jewish Christians as a defined group. Their critique does not overturn the broader consensus that John 9 reflects some real, later conflict between the Johannine community and synagogue authorities — only the specific mechanism Martyn proposed for it, a caution mainstream scholarship has increasingly absorbed.

130 — A play in seven scenes

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), introductory comments to chapter 9. Brown reads John 9 as a deliberately constructed drama unfolding across seven distinct scenes — Jesus and his disciples, the healing itself, the neighbors' dispute over the man's identity, the first Pharisaic hearing, the summoning of the parents, the second and final hearing ending in expulsion, and Jesus's return to find the man — with the healed man's own testimony growing steadily bolder at each turn while his interrogators' position hardens in the opposite direction. Brown treats the structure as a genuine piece of literary artistry, not merely a sequence of loosely connected reports.

131 — Opened eyes as a messianic-age signal

Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, 2 vols. (Peabody, MA: Hendrickson Publishers, 2003), commentary ad loc. John 9:1-7, background discussion. Keener connects the healing to Isaiah's prophetic association of the messianic age with restored sight — "the eyes of the blind shall be opened" (Isaiah 35:5) and "to open the eyes that are blind" (Isaiah 42:7) — background that would have given a first-century Jewish audience reason to read a blind man's healing as a deliberate, theologically loaded sign rather than a bare wonder-story, in keeping with the Fourth Gospel's own consistent practice of presenting Jesus's healings as signs pointing beyond themselves.

132 — The sixth sign, and a hypothesized earlier source

Robert T. Fortna, The Fourth Gospel and Its Predecessor: From Narrative Source to Present Gospel (Philadelphia: Fortress Press, 1988), reconstruction of the Signs Gospel's narrative sequence. This healing occupies the sixth position in the traditional count of the Fourth Gospel's seven numbered signs, and Fortna's source-critical reconstruction of a hypothesized earlier Signs Gospel underlying John includes a version of this same narrative among its recovered episodes, consistent with the broader scholarly view that this chapter's core healing account is one of the Gospel's oldest traditional layers, onto which the lengthy interrogation scenes were subsequently built.

133 — The shepherd image's real background: royal failure, not pastoral idyll

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 10:1-18. Brown traces the chapter's shepherd imagery to a specific Hebrew Bible tradition rather than a generic pastoral idyll: Ezekiel 34's oracle against Israel's own failed shepherd-kings, who feed themselves instead of the flock and let the sheep scatter and become prey; Psalm 23's portrait of the LORD as the psalmist's own shepherd; and Numbers 27:17, where Moses asks that Israel not be left 'like sheep without a shepherd' after his death. Read against that background, Jesus's claim to be the good shepherd is a claim to succeed where Israel's own royal shepherds failed, not merely a comforting rural image.

134 — One discourse or two, spliced together?

Rudolf Bultmann, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, trans. G.R. Beasley-Murray et al. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1971; German original 1941), commentary on John 10:1-21. Bultmann's source-critical analysis proposed that John 10 preserves two originally separate shepherd sayings-units awkwardly joined by the evangelist: a gate saying (10:1-10, where Jesus is the entrance the sheep pass through) and a shepherd saying (10:11-18, where Jesus is the one who tends and dies for the flock), each with its own internal logic, spliced into a single discourse whose controlling image shifts without warning.

135 — Or one discourse, deliberately layered

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 10:1-21. Brown argues the shift from gate to shepherd is not evidence of clumsy editorial splicing but a deliberate rhetorical technique attested elsewhere in John, such as the water and living-water and the bread and bread-of-life double images in chapters 4 and 6: the evangelist layers a second figure onto the first once the first has made its point, rather than abandoning it outright. The disagreement between this reading and a source-division proposal remains a live one in Johannine scholarship, not a settled question.

136 — The gate that is also the shepherd

C.K. Barrett, The Gospel According to St John: An Introduction with Commentary and Notes on the Greek Text, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1978), commentary ad loc. John 10:7-11. Barrett notes the plain grammatical oddity that within a few verses Jesus is identified first as 'the gate for the sheep' (10:7, 9) and then as 'the good shepherd' (10:11, 14) who enters by that same gate (10:2) — two roles that, taken as a single continuous picture, do not quite cohere, since the shepherd would then be entering through himself.

137 — Sheep not yet gathered

D. Moody Smith, The Theology of the Gospel of John, New Testament Theology series (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1995), chapter on the Good Shepherd discourse; see also Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 10:16. Jesus's line about 'other sheep that are not of this fold,' destined to be gathered into 'one flock, one shepherd,' is widely read by mainstream commentators as a reference to the Gentile mission of the early church, a mission not underway in Jesus's own lifetime. The saying is generally treated as a retrospective theological statement, composed after the fact and placed on Jesus's lips by the evangelist, rather than a claim the historical Jesus is likely to have voiced in these terms before his death.

138 — Words a movement grows into its founder's mouth

Bart D. Ehrman, The New Testament: A Historical Introduction to the Early Christian Writings, 7th ed. (New York: Oxford University Press, 2019), chapter on the Gospel of John. Ehrman describes a broader pattern historical-critical scholarship applies across the Fourth Gospel: theological convictions the Johannine community reached only after Jesus's death and resurrection, including its stance on a Gentile mission the historical Jesus is not otherwise recorded as having launched, are voiced in the narrative as things Jesus said during his ministry, a retrojection technique consistent with ancient biographical writing generally.

139 — The Feast's own origin story

1 Maccabees 4:36-59 and 2 Maccabees 10:1-8 (Apocrypha/Deuterocanonical books), the two earliest surviving accounts of the Temple's rededication. Both describe Judas Maccabeus and his forces cleansing and rededicating the Jerusalem Temple altar in 164 BCE, three years to the day after Antiochus IV Epiphanes had it desecrated, and instituting an eight-day annual festival of light and sacrifice in commemoration — the historical event John's 'Feast of Dedication' (10:22) names.

140 — Josephus on the desecration and its undoing

Flavius Josephus, Jewish Antiquities, Book 12, chapters 7.6-7 (sections 316-326) (Loeb Classical Library, trans. Ralph Marcus, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press). Josephus, writing over two centuries after the events, narrates Antiochus IV Epiphanes's desecration of the Jerusalem Temple, including the installation of a pagan altar over the altar of burnt offering, and Judas Maccabeus's subsequent recapture and rededication of the Temple, explicitly naming the resulting festival 'Lights,' because the right of worship had unexpectedly reappeared to the Jewish people 'beyond their hopes.'

141 — The oil-miracle story: absent from the earliest sources

Babylonian Talmud, tractate Shabbat, folio 21b (Soncino translation; standard critical editions). The now-famous legend that a single day's supply of ritually pure oil burned for eight days in the rededicated Temple appears in neither 1 Maccabees nor 2 Maccabees nor Josephus; it surfaces for the first time centuries later in this Talmudic discussion, making it a later theological elaboration on a genuinely historical rededication rather than part of the feast's earliest attested memory.

142 — A season the Gospel gets right

Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, vol. 1 (Peabody, MA: Hendrickson Publishers, 2003), commentary ad loc. John 10:22-23. Keener notes that the Feast of Dedication falls in the Jewish month of Kislev, corresponding to late November-December, and that John's aside 'it was winter' is both historically accurate for that festival's fixed calendar date and consistent with the narrative detail that Jesus is walking in the portico of Solomon, a covered colonnade rather than an open court, exactly where a person would seek shelter from winter weather in Jerusalem.

143 — Solomon's Portico as an attested structure

Flavius Josephus, Jewish Antiquities, Book 20, section 221 (Loeb Classical Library, trans. Louis H. Feldman, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press); compare Jewish War, Book 5, sections 184-227, on the Temple's porticoes generally. Josephus describes the Temple's eastern portico as a surviving remnant of Solomon's original construction, standing along the eastern edge of the Temple platform, an architectural feature independently attested outside the Gospels and giving John's setting detail a concrete, verifiable referent rather than an invented backdrop.

144 — An argument from the lesser to the greater

D.A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans / Leicester: Inter-Varsity Press, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 10:34-36. Carson identifies Jesus's appeal to Psalm 82:6 as a recognizable rabbinic argument form, qal va-chomer, 'light and heavy,' reasoning from a lesser case to a greater one: if scripture itself can apply the word 'gods' to mere human recipients of God's word without that being blasphemy, the argument runs, then it is not blasphemy for the one whom the Father consecrated and sent into the world to call himself God's Son — a real, attested form of Jewish scriptural reasoning, not an improvised deflection.

145 — Psalm 82's gods, read as human judges

Marvin E. Tate, Psalms 51-100, Word Biblical Commentary vol. 20 (Dallas: Word Books, 1990), commentary ad loc. Psalm 82. Tate surveys the majority historical-critical reading of Psalm 82, in which the 'gods' (elohim) addressed and condemned by God for judging unjustly are best understood as human judges or rulers of Israel and the nations, addressed with an honorific divine title because they exercise God-delegated judicial authority — the reading John's Jesus appeals to in arguing that scripture itself calls certain humans 'gods' without impiety.

146 — Or read as a divine council

Michael S. Heiser, 'The Divine Council in Late Canonical and Non-Canonical Second Temple Jewish Literature' (Ph.D. dissertation, University of Wisconsin-Madison, 2004), chapter on Psalm 82. Heiser argues for a minority but well-represented scholarly reading in which Psalm 82's 'gods' (elohim) are not human judges at all but literal members of Yahweh's heavenly divine council, condemned to 'die like men' (82:7) for failing to administer justice among the nations under their charge, a reading with roots in comparative ancient Near Eastern divine-council literature and in some strands of Second Temple Jewish angelology. The two readings remain a genuine, unresolved exegetical dispute; John's Jesus does not adjudicate between them, only exploits the term's flexibility for his own argument.

147 — A pronoun that splits the manuscripts

Bruce M. Metzger, A Textual Commentary on the Greek New Testament, 2nd ed. (Stuttgart: Deutsche Bibelgesellschaft / United Bible Societies, 1994), commentary ad loc. John 10:29. Metzger documents a genuine, long-recognized textual crux in Jesus's claim about the Father who has given him the sheep: the earliest manuscripts split between a masculine reading, emphasizing that the Father himself is greater than all, and a neuter reading, emphasizing the magnitude of the gift itself, a variant significant enough that the committee could not resolve it with full confidence beyond a marginal preference — a reminder that even a verse read as a clean statement of divine protection rests on a disputed word in the Greek manuscript tradition.

148 — A discourse shaped by a later synagogue conflict

Urban C. von Wahlde, The Gospel and Letters of John, vol. 2: Commentary on the Gospel of John, Eerdmans Critical Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 2010), commentary on John 10:19-21 and the repeated hostile exchanges with 'the Jews' across chapters 5 through 10. Von Wahlde reads the discourse's recurring blasphemy accusations and attempted stonings as bearing the marks of the Johannine community's own later conflict with synagogue authorities near the end of the first century, projected back onto narrated exchanges between Jesus and his contemporaries decades earlier.

149 — One in what sense?

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 10:30. Brown notes that the Greek of 'I and the Father are one' uses the neuter hen, 'one thing,' not the masculine heis, 'one person,' and argues the original sense in context is a unity of will and protective power over the flock, consistent with the surrounding verses about no one snatching the sheep away, rather than a fully articulated claim of metaphysical identity of being. The later, doctrinally freighted reading the phrase acquired in fourth-century trinitarian debate is a theological development built on, rather than already stated by, the verse itself.

150 — Back to where it started

Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, vol. 1 (Peabody, MA: Hendrickson Publishers, 2003), commentary ad loc. John 10:40-42. Keener reads Jesus's withdrawal across the Jordan to the place where John had been baptizing as a deliberate literary inclusio bracketing the entire public-ministry section of the Gospel, returning the narrative to the same Transjordan location named at its opening (1:28), with the local crowd's verdict there, that everything John said about Jesus was true, serving as the evangelist's own closing argument for John the Baptist's reliability as a witness.

151 — A delay explained as theology, not neglect

D.A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 11:4-6. Carson and the mainstream commentary tradition read Jesus's declaration that the illness is "for the glory of God" and his deliberate two-day delay (11:6) as a programmatic Johannine framing device rather than a report of ordinary human hesitation: the narrator wants the reader to see the coming sign, before it happens, as revelatory rather than accidental, the same pattern already established at the healing of the man born blind (9:3).

152 — Fifteen stadia from Jerusalem

Jerome Murphy-O'Connor, The Holy Land: An Oxford Archaeological Guide from Earliest Times to 1700, 5th ed. (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2008), entry on Bethany. John's own geographical note that Bethany was "about two miles" (literally fifteen stadia) from Jerusalem (11:18) matches the real distance to the village now called al-Eizariya ("place of Lazarus" in Arabic), on the eastern slope of the Mount of Olives; Murphy-O'Connor's guide catalogs a tradition, attested from at least the fourth century, locating Lazarus's tomb there, though the tomb shown to pilgrims today has been substantially rebuilt and altered since antiquity.

153 — Thomas before Thomas is famous for doubting

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 11:16. Brown notes that Thomas's "Let us also go, that we may die with him" is the character's first speaking appearance in the Gospel, establishing a loyal-but-uncomprehending fatalism well before his more famous scene of doubt at the resurrection appearances (20:24-29); commentators generally read the two scenes together as a single, consistent Johannine character arc rather than as unrelated incidents.

154 — Why the fourth day mattered

Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, vol. 2 (Peabody, MA: Hendrickson Publishers, 2003), commentary ad loc. John 11:39. Keener catalogs the later rabbinic tradition, recorded in sources including Leviticus Rabbah 18:1 and Ecclesiastes Rabbah 12:6, that a departed soul was believed to linger near its body for three days hoping to return, after which the body was understood to begin visible decay and any hope of resuscitation was considered closed; John's insistence that Lazarus had been dead four full days (11:17, 11:39) functions within this popular belief-world to rule out any suspicion of resuscitation from a merely apparent death.

155 — A confession usually given to Peter

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 11:27. Brown observes that Martha's declaration, "I believe that you are the Christ, the Son of God, who is coming into the world," is couched in language strikingly close to the confession the Synoptic Gospels give to Peter at Caesarea Philippi (Matthew 16:16); rather than treating this as coincidence, the mainstream commentary tradition reads it as a deliberate Johannine choice to place the Gospel's clearest full christological confession in the mouth of a woman, ahead of the male disciples.

156 — Two different words for weeping in the same scene

C.K. Barrett, The Gospel according to St John: An Introduction with Commentary and Notes on the Greek Text, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1978), commentary ad loc. John 11:33, 35. Barrett notes that the Greek text uses two different verbs for weeping within the space of a few verses: Mary and the accompanying mourners are described with klaio, the ordinary word for audible, public lamentation (11:33), while Jesus's own response two verses later uses the rarer dakryo, a quieter, more restrained word for shedding tears (11:35) — the shortest verse in most English translations. The word choice is not incidental; commentators debate whether it signals private grief held apart from the surrounding public wailing, or simply variatio, an evangelist's stylistic avoidance of repeating the same verb twice.

157 — Anger, revulsion, or grief? A disputed verb

C.K. Barrett, The Gospel according to St John: An Introduction with Commentary and Notes on the Greek Text, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1978), commentary ad loc. John 11:33, 38. The verb translated "deeply moved" (embrimaomai) ordinarily carries a connotation of anger, snorting, or indignation rather than sorrow, and John uses it twice in this scene (11:33, 38); Barrett and the wider commentary tradition are divided over whether the emotion in view is Jesus's anger at unbelief and at death's power itself, or whether the term has been softened here toward simple emotional agitation — a genuine translation and interpretive crux the English "deeply moved in spirit and troubled" only partly resolves.

158 — John's greatest miracle, absent from Mark, Matthew, and Luke

Rudolf Bultmann, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, trans. G.R. Beasley-Murray (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1971), commentary on John 11:1-44; see also Bart D. Ehrman, Jesus, Interrupted: Revealing the Hidden Contradictions in the Bible (New York: HarperOne, 2009), chapter on the differences among the Gospels. Bultmann reads the Lazarus narrative as a theological composition built by the evangelist around inherited symbolic material rather than a report of an actual event, and Ehrman treats its total absence from Mark, Matthew, and Luke — despite being, on John's own telling, the single event that precipitates the formal decision to kill Jesus (11:53) — as a strong indicator that the story did not circulate in the tradition available to the Synoptic authors.

159 — Silence is not, by itself, proof

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), introductory discussion preceding the commentary on John 11. Brown, more cautiously, argues that an argument from Synoptic silence is not on its own decisive: the Synoptic Gospels' coverage of Jesus's Judean ministry, as opposed to the Galilean material that dominates Mark and the bulk of Matthew and Luke, is comparatively thin to begin with, and each evangelist selects and omits material according to distinct compositional purposes, so the absence of a Judean-based story from Galilee-centered sources need not by itself indicate the story was invented.

160 — The same name, twice, in two different genres

C.K. Barrett, The Gospel according to St John: An Introduction with Commentary and Notes on the Greek Text, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1978), commentary ad loc. John 11:1. Barrett notes the striking coincidence that the only other Lazarus named anywhere in the canonical Gospels is the beggar in Jesus's parable of the rich man and Lazarus (Luke 16:19-31) — a parable that itself turns on a rich man begging that Lazarus be sent back from the dead to warn his brothers, and being refused, since "they will not be convinced even if someone rises from the dead" (Luke 16:31). Some scholars, Bultmann among them, have proposed the Johannine narrative developed out of this Lukan parable material; others regard the shared name as coincidental, since Lazarus (from the Hebrew Eleazar, "God has helped") was a common contemporary Jewish name.

161 — A hypothetical earlier miracle-collection

Robert T. Fortna, The Fourth Gospel and Its Predecessor: From Narrative Source to Present Gospel (Philadelphia: Fortress Press, 1988). Fortna's reconstruction of a hypothetical pre-Johannine "Signs Source" — a numbered collection of miracle stories the evangelist is theorized to have inherited and then reworked with extended theological discourse — treats the Lazarus story as a strong candidate for inclusion in that earlier source, on the theory that a core miracle account circulated independently before John's surrounding discourse material was added around it; the hypothesis remains widely discussed but is not a consensus position.

162 — The most sustained scholarly argument on the question

John P. Meier, A Marginal Jew: Rethinking the Historical Jesus, Volume Two: Mentor, Message, and Miracles (New York: Doubleday, 1994), excursus on the raising of Lazarus. Meier devotes an extended, methodical treatment to the Lazarus narrative specifically because of its evidential weight for historical-Jesus research, concluding that while some substratum tradition involving Jesus and a resuscitation-type action at Bethany plausibly lies behind the story, the developed narrative as it stands in John 11 — with its extended dialogue, staged four-day delay, and explicit causal link to the crucifixion — bears heavy marks of Johannine theological composition, making any confident historical reconstruction of the underlying event unusually difficult.

163 — 'By this time there will be a bad odor'

Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, vol. 2 (Peabody, MA: Hendrickson Publishers, 2003), commentary ad loc. John 11:39. Keener reads Martha's blunt objection to opening the tomb — rendered "Lord, by this time there will be a bad odor, for he has been dead four days," or, in the King James Version's memorably direct phrasing, "he stinketh" — as a detail placed in the narrative specifically to close off any reading of Lazarus's death as a coma, swoon, or premature burial; the text goes out of its way to insist, through its own characters, that ordinary decomposition had already begun.

164 — An unusually long eighteen-year tenure

Flavius Josephus, Jewish Antiquities, Book 18, sections 33-35 and 95 (Loeb Classical Library, trans. Louis H. Feldman, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 1965). Josephus records that Joseph Caiaphas was appointed high priest by the Roman prefect Valerius Gratus around 18 CE and was removed from office by the legate Vitellius around 36 CE, a roughly eighteen-year tenure that is strikingly long by the standard of the office in this period, when high priests were typically replaced every few years; historians generally read the length of Caiaphas's tenure as reflecting a stable, cooperative working relationship with Rome, spanning the entirety of Pontius Pilate's own prefecture (26-36 CE).

165 — A named ossuary from a first-century tomb

Zvi Greenhut, 'Burial Cave of the Caiaphas Family,' Biblical Archaeology Review 18, no. 5 (1992): 29-36. In 1990, construction work in Jerusalem's Peace Forest (North Talpiot) uncovered a first-century burial cave containing twelve ossuaries, one of them elaborately decorated and inscribed twice with the name "Yehosef bar Qafa" (Joseph, son of Caiaphas); Greenhut, who directed the excavation, and the broader scholarly consensus that followed identify the ossuary as very likely belonging to the high priest named in the Gospels, making it a rare case of probable direct archaeological corroboration of a named New Testament figure.

166 — A prophecy the prophet doesn't know he's making

R. Alan Culpepper, Anatomy of the Fourth Gospel: A Study in Literary Design (Philadelphia: Fortress Press, 1983), discussion of Johannine irony. Culpepper's landmark literary study of the Fourth Gospel treats the narrator's explicit aside at 11:51-52 — that Caiaphas "did not say this of his own accord" but "prophesied" that Jesus would die for the nation, and not for the nation only, without understanding the deeper truth of his own words — as one of the clearest instances of the Gospel's recurring irony device, in which a character's words carry a true meaning invisible to the speaker but plainly signaled to the reader by the narrator standing outside the scene.

167 — The same plot, a different trigger

Mark 14:1-2 and Matthew 26:3-5 record chief priests and elders (Matthew specifically naming Caiaphas's residence) conspiring to arrest and kill Jesus in the days before Passover, but neither Synoptic account gives any reason for the timing beyond general hostility and fear of a crowd disturbance during the feast. John's version supplies a specific narrative trigger — the raising of Lazarus and the popular response it provokes (11:45-48) — that is entirely absent from the Synoptic accounts of the same underlying decision, a further data point in the wider debate over how much of John's causal framework for the Passion reflects independent historical memory versus the evangelist's own narrative architecture.

168 — A retreat to an uncertain village

D.A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 11:54. Carson notes that the Ephraim to which Jesus withdraws is most often identified with the modern village of et-Taiyibeh, roughly twenty kilometers northeast of Jerusalem near the edge of the Judean wilderness, an identification going back to Eusebius's fourth-century Onomasticon; the site has not been independently confirmed by excavation specific to this Gospel event, and some caution is warranted before treating the identification as more than a well-informed ancient guess.

169 — Real Roman-era anxiety behind the council's fear

Paula Fredriksen, Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews: A Jewish Life and the Emergence of Christianity (New York: Alfred A. Knopf, 1999). Fredriksen situates the Sanhedrin's stated fear in this chapter — "If we let him go on like this, everyone will believe in him, and the Romans will come and take away both our place and our nation" (11:48) — within the real, well-documented Roman practice of holding local ruling councils responsible for maintaining order during pilgrimage festivals, when Jerusalem's population swelled and any crowd disturbance around a popular figure carried a genuine risk of Roman military intervention and reprisal against the city's governing elite.

170 — A late link, added to bind two halves of the Gospel

Urban C. von Wahlde, The Gospel and Letters of John, vol. 2, Eerdmans Critical Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 2010), commentary on John 11. Von Wahlde's compositional-stratigraphy analysis of the Fourth Gospel places the Lazarus narrative among the latest material added to the text, arguing that its heavy causal weight — explicitly supplying the reason the authorities decide to kill Jesus (11:53) — was assigned to the passage precisely because it was composed, at a late editorial stage, to bridge the Gospel's 'Book of Signs' (chapters 1-12) to its Passion narrative, rather than surviving intact from an early layer of tradition.

171 — The anointing at Simon the leper's house

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 12:1-8. Brown lays the Johannine anointing directly alongside Mark 14:3-9 and its close parallel Matthew 26:6-13, where an unnamed woman anoints Jesus's head (not his feet) with costly ointment in the house of Simon the leper at Bethany, and unnamed disciples (Matthew) or those present (Mark) object to the waste; John's version keeps the place-name Bethany and the price estimate but relocates the anointing to the feet, names the woman as Mary, and narrows the objection to a single named disciple, Judas.

172 — A third anointing, earlier and elsewhere

Joseph A. Fitzmyer, The Gospel According to Luke I-IX, Anchor Bible vol. 28 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1981), commentary ad loc. Luke 7:36-50. Luke places a woman's anointing of Jesus's feet at a different point in the ministry entirely, during the Galilean period rather than the run-up to the final Passover, in the house of a Pharisee named Simon rather than Bethany, and makes the woman's tears and her unnamed sinfulness, not burial preparation, the point of the story, which ends in a pronouncement about forgiveness rather than a comment on Jesus's coming death.

173 — One tradition, two, or three?

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), excursus on the anointing accounts, commentary ad loc. John 12:1-8. Brown lays out, without forcing a verdict, the genuine range of scholarly positions: that all three (or four, counting Mark and Matthew separately) accounts stem from a single underlying incident retold with diverging details; that two originally distinct traditions, a Bethany burial-anointing and a Galilean sinful-woman story, have been partly conflated in transmission; or that the evangelists preserve three genuinely separate incidents in Jesus's ministry. The name (Mary), the place (Bethany), and the theme (burial) that John shares with Mark and Matthew but not Luke are the strongest arguments for treating John and the Synoptic Bethany story as variants of one tradition, while Luke's version is the hardest of the three to reconcile with the others.

174 — What exactly did Jesus tell her to do?

Bruce M. Metzger, A Textual Commentary on the Greek New Testament, 2nd ed. (Stuttgart: Deutsche Bibelgesellschaft / United Bible Societies, 1994), ad loc. John 12:7; see also the Nestle-Aland Novum Testamentum Graece textual apparatus at the same verse. The Greek of Jesus's reply to Judas is genuinely awkward, a construction that can be rendered either as a command to let Mary keep the remaining perfume for a future burial, or as a statement that she has, in effect, already kept and used it for that purpose; translations split accordingly, comparing the King James Version's `against the day of my burying hath she kept this'' with the New Revised Standard Version's `she bought it so that she might keep it for the day of my burial.'' Both the grammar and the manuscript tradition leave room for more than one defensible English rendering.

175 — A thief, by the narrator's own account

Bart D. Ehrman, The New Testament: A Historical Introduction to the Early Christian Writings, 7th ed. (New York: Oxford University Press, 2019), chapter on the Gospel of John. Ehrman notes that John's Gospel consistently sharpens Judas's characterization well beyond anything in the Synoptics: Mark and Matthew's parallel scene has unnamed disciples object to the waste with no suggestion of dishonesty, while John alone names Judas as the objector and alone supplies the narrator's aside that he raised the objection `not because he cared about the poor, but because he was a thief, and having charge of the moneybag he used to help himself to what was put into it'' (12:6). No Synoptic source shares this detail, which reads as a distinctively Johannine narrative and theological choice, deepening Judas's villainy well before the betrayal itself, rather than an independently attested strand of shared early tradition.

176 — The word for the box itself

C.K. Barrett, The Gospel According to St John: An Introduction with Commentary and Notes on the Greek Text, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1978), commentary ad loc. John 12:6 and 13:29. The Greek term John uses, glossokomon, originally denoted a small case for the reed mouthpieces of wind instruments before broadening to mean any small box or chest; John uses it twice for the group's common purse, once here and again at 13:29, the only two occurrences of the word anywhere in the New Testament, both tied to Judas's custodianship of it.

177 — A citation, not a straight quotation

Carol L. Meyers and Eric M. Meyers, Haggai, Zechariah 9-14: A New Translation with Introduction and Commentary, Anchor Bible vol. 25C (New York: Doubleday, 1993), commentary ad loc. Zechariah 9:9. John's citation (`Fear not, daughter of Zion; behold, your king is coming, sitting on a donkey's colt,'' 12:15) is not a verbatim reproduction of Zechariah 9:9 in either the Hebrew or the Greek Septuagint text: the opening `fear not'' is not in Zechariah 9:9 at all (it echoes Zephaniah 3:16 and Isaiah 40:9 instead), while Zechariah's own fuller line describing the king as righteous, victorious, and humble is dropped. The Meyerses' commentary situates Zechariah 9:9 within a Persian-period vision of a peaceable, non-military royal entry, a king riding a donkey rather than a war-horse, which is precisely the contrast John's citation is built to draw out.

178 — One animal or two?

D.A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans / Leicester: Inter-Varsity Press, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 12:14-15. Mark, Luke, and John all describe Jesus mounting a single animal, a young donkey or colt. Matthew's version (21:2-7), read very literally, has Jesus riding two animals at once, a donkey and a colt together, which mainstream commentary widely explains as Matthew woodenly literalizing the synonymous Hebrew poetic parallelism of Zechariah 9:9 (`on a donkey, on a colt, the foal of a donkey''), a single animal described twice in two parallel phrases, into two separate animals. John's version, like Mark's and Luke's, keeps to one.

179 — Why historians take some version of this seriously

E.P. Sanders, Jesus and Judaism (Philadelphia: Fortress Press, 1985), chapter on the Temple action and its aftermath. Sanders, whose own primary argument concerns Jesus's action in the Temple rather than the entry itself, treats some kind of deliberate, symbolically loaded arrival in Jerusalem shortly before Passover as historically plausible, precisely because it helps explain, in ordinary cause-and-effect terms, why the authorities moved against Jesus at that specific festival rather than at any other point in his ministry: a public gesture readable as a messianic claim, arriving in a city swollen with Passover pilgrims under close Roman and priestly watch, supplies exactly the kind of provocation historians look for to make the arrest and execution intelligible as a real sequence of events rather than a purely theological construction.

180 — How big were Passover crowds, really?

Flavius Josephus, The Jewish War, Book 6, section 9.3 (paragraphs 420-425) (Loeb Classical Library, trans. H. St. J. Thackeray, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press). Josephus reports a census taken under the procurator Cestius by counting paschal lambs sacrificed at a single Passover and multiplying by the minimum party size required to eat one, arriving at a figure historians overwhelmingly regard as rhetorical exaggeration rather than a literal headcount; even discounted heavily, the account confirms that Jerusalem's Passover pilgrim crowds were, by any standard, enormous, which is the relevant backdrop for weighing how large and how visible a public entry into the city plausibly could have been.

181 — Amplification is also a real possibility

John P. Meier, A Marginal Jew: Rethinking the Historical Jesus, Volume Two: Mentor, Message, and Miracles (New York: Doubleday, 1994), discussion of criteria for assessing crowd reactions in the Gospel tradition. Meier cautions that while some public entry into Jerusalem before Passover is plausible, the specific scale and unanimity of crowd acclaim described in all four Gospels, palm branches, a whole city's welcome, explicit royal and messianic titles shouted aloud, may reflect the Evangelists' retrospective theological amplification of a smaller, more ambiguous event, since a demonstration of the scale described would itself have been a natural trigger for immediate Roman intervention, which none of the Gospels reports happening on the spot.

182 — A complaint that turns out to be true

Urban C. von Wahlde, The Gospel and Letters of John, Volume 2: Commentary on the Gospel of John, Eerdmans Critical Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 2010), commentary ad loc. John 12:19. Von Wahlde reads the Pharisees' exasperated `the world has gone after him'' as a characteristic piece of Johannine irony: characters within the story speak truer than they know, since the scene that follows immediately, Greeks from beyond Israel asking to see Jesus, begins to make good, quite literally, on the complaint the Pharisees intend only as hyperbole.

183 — Who were the Greeks at the feast?

Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, vol. 2 (Peabody, MA: Hendrickson Publishers, 2003), commentary ad loc. John 12:20-22. Keener identifies the Greeks (Hellenes, not Hellenists, i.e. not Greek-speaking Jews) most plausibly as Gentile God-fearers or proselytes attending the Jerusalem festival, sympathizers with Judaism drawn to its monotheism and ethics who worshiped at the Temple's outer precincts without full conversion, comparable to figures like the centurion Cornelius in Acts 10. Their appearance is not best read as an anachronistic first-century missionary scene planted into Jesus's own lifetime, but as a narrative signal, timed deliberately by the Evangelist, that the hour about to be announced concerns a mission reaching beyond Israel.

184 — The hour, finally arrived

Rudolf Bultmann, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, trans. G.R. Beasley-Murray et al. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1971; German original 1941), commentary ad loc. John 12:23. Bultmann traces the hour as one of John's principal structural motifs, repeatedly deferred earlier in the Gospel (`his hour had not yet come,'' 2:4, 7:30, 8:20) and here, for the first time, declared to have arrived; Bultmann and much subsequent commentary treat this declaration as the hinge on which the Gospel's whole architecture turns, from a Book of Signs addressed to the world into the Passion material addressed to the disciples.

185 — A saying with Synoptic cousins

John P. Meier, A Marginal Jew: Rethinking the Historical Jesus, Volume Two: Mentor, Message, and Miracles (New York: Doubleday, 1994), discussion of the paradoxical life-and-death sayings; compare D. Moody Smith, John, Abingdon New Testament Commentaries (Nashville: Abingdon Press, 1999), commentary ad loc. John 12:24-25. The saying `whoever loves his life loses it, and whoever hates his life in this world will keep it for eternal life'' (12:25) has clear, independently worded counterparts in the Synoptic tradition: `whoever would save his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life for my sake... will save it'' (Mark 8:35; similarly Matthew 16:25, Luke 9:24) and `whoever finds his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life for my sake will find it'' (Matthew 10:39; Luke 17:33). Smith treats a saying attested across John and multiple Synoptic settings with different wording as a meaningful mark of an early, durable tradition, even though John alone attaches it to the image of a grain of wheat falling into the earth.

186 — A heavenly voice, out of season

C.K. Barrett, The Gospel According to St John: An Introduction with Commentary and Notes on the Greek Text, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1978), commentary ad loc. John 12:28-30; compare Rudolf Bultmann, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, trans. G.R. Beasley-Murray et al. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1971), ad loc. the same verses. John's Gospel narrates neither a heavenly voice at Jesus's baptism (contrast Mark 1:11 and parallels) nor a Transfiguration scene at all (contrast Mark 9:2-8 and parallels), the two moments where the Synoptics do record a voice from heaven; this is the only place in John where one occurs. Barrett and Bultmann both note the scene's structural resemblance to the Synoptic voice-from-heaven episodes and have suggested John may preserve a compressed or relocated echo of that same broader tradition rather than an unrelated, freestanding Johannine invention, though the comparison remains a resemblance, not a proven literary dependence.

187 — Who has believed our report

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John I-XII, Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary ad loc. John 12:37-38. The narrator's first proof-text for persistent unbelief despite the signs is Isaiah 53:1 (`Lord, who has believed what he heard from us, and to whom has the arm of the Lord been revealed?''), drawn from the same Suffering Servant poem (Isaiah 52:13-53:12) that early Christian writers elsewhere read as predicting a suffering messiah; Paul quotes the identical verse for a comparable purpose at Romans 10:16, evidence that this specific Isaian text had an established use in early Christian argument about Israel's unbelief well before or alongside John's own use of it.

188 — Blinded eyes, hardened hearts

D.A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans / Leicester: Inter-Varsity Press, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 12:39-41. The second citation, Isaiah 6:10 (`He has blinded their eyes and hardened their heart... lest they see with their eyes and understand with their heart, and turn, and I would heal them''), is among the most frequently reused Old Testament texts anywhere in the New Testament for explaining unbelief; it also appears at Mark 4:12 and parallels, and again at Acts 28:26-27 and Romans 11:8, making it one of the clearest examples in the New Testament of a single prophetic text put to sustained theological work across independent authors and settings, each reading Israel's own scripture as having anticipated its own resistance to the gospel.

189 — Whose fear of expulsion, Jesus's contemporaries or John's?

J. Louis Martyn, History and Theology in the Fourth Gospel, 3rd ed. (Louisville, KY: Westminster John Knox Press, 2003; originally published 1968), foundational study of the Gospel's two-level drama. Martyn's influential and widely debated thesis reads the note that `many even of the authorities believed in him, but... did not confess it... for fear that they would be put out of the synagogue'' (12:42) as reflecting not only, or even primarily, the situation of Jesus's own lifetime, but the later, harder conflict between John's own late-first-century Christian community and the local synagogue from which it had been or was being expelled, a conflict John is thought to project back onto the narrated events of Jesus's ministry. Subsequent scholarship has nuanced and in places challenged the specifics of Martyn's reconstruction, but the basic observation that this verse speaks to a real, painful separation between synagogue and church remains widely shared.

190 — Whose job was it to wash feet?

John Christopher Thomas, Footwashing in John 13 and the Johannine Community, Journal for the Study of the New Testament Supplement Series 61 (Sheffield: Sheffield Academic Press, 1991), chapter 1. Thomas surveys the ancient evidence and finds foot washing was ordinarily a servant's task performed for arriving guests (compare Genesis 18:4; 19:2; 24:32; 43:24), one a host without slaves might instead leave the guest to do alone, or that a wife or child might perform for a husband or father within a household; Abigail's self-description as ready to `wash the feet of the servants of my lord'' (1 Samuel 25:41) marks it as an act of extreme self-abasement, and its omission is noted as a conspicuous social slight in Luke 7:44. A teacher washing his own disciples' feet inverted every expectation the custom carried.

191 — The one task even a disciple was excused from

Babylonian Talmud, Ketubbot 96a (Soncino Talmud translation), discussed in relation to John 13 by, among others, Craig S. Keener, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, vol. 2 (Peabody, MA: Hendrickson Publishers, 2003), commentary ad loc. John 13:1-11. The passage holds that a disciple should perform for his teacher every service a slave performs for a master, with one exception: untying the master's sandal. Keener and other commentators cite this as evidence that foot-related menial service sat at the very bottom of the status ladder, low enough that even the disciple-teacher relationship, itself modeled on slavery, drew the line there; Jesus's act in John 13 does not merely lower him to a servant's task but to one beneath what his own students would have owed him.

192 — A sacramental echo in 'no share with me'?

Rudolf Bultmann, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, trans. G.R. Beasley-Murray (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1971; German original 1941), commentary on John 13:6-11; compare Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John (XIII-XXI), Anchor Bible vol. 29A (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1970), commentary ad loc. Bultmann reads Jesus's warning that Peter `has no share with me'' unless washed as carrying baptismal overtones layered onto the hospitality scene, part of his broader source-critical reconstruction of an ecclesiastical redactor's hand in the Fourth Gospel. Brown weighs the same possibility more cautiously, noting the verse also works as a plain statement about communion with Jesus in this specific moment without requiring a later sacramental gloss; the two readings are not mutually exclusive, and mainstream commentary remains divided on how much weight the sacramental reading deserves.

193 — A short reading with a long theological reach

Bruce M. Metzger, A Textual Commentary on the Greek New Testament, 2nd ed. (Stuttgart: Deutsche Bibelgesellschaft / United Bible Societies, 1994), commentary on John 13:10. Several early witnesses lack the clause `except for his feet'' in Jesus's statement that the one who has bathed needs no further washing `except for his feet, but is completely clean'' (13:10), leaving only a flatter reading in which the bathed person needs no washing at all. The shorter reading, if original, removes any suggestion that foot washing itself carries continuing ritual necessity for someone already clean; the modern critical preference for the longer reading is a committee judgment call rather than manuscript near-unanimity, and the variant is regularly cited in textual-criticism literature as a case where a single clause carries real theological weight.

194 — A maxim that travels

John P. Meier, A Marginal Jew: Rethinking the Historical Jesus, Volume One: The Roots of the Problem and the Person (New York: Doubleday, 1991), discussion of the criteria of authenticity, especially multiple attestation. Jesus's closing maxim that `a servant is not greater than his master, nor is a messenger greater than the one who sent him'' (John 13:16) has independent parallels in Matthew 10:24 and Luke 6:40, each embedded in a different literary setting. Meier's standard treatment of multiple attestation treats sayings that surface this way, detached from any single narrative frame, as strong candidates for genuinely early, freely circulating tradition, distinct from material that reads as one evangelist's own composition.

195 — A figure who appears here for the first time

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John (XIII-XXI), Anchor Bible vol. 29A (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1970), commentary ad loc. John 13:23; R. Alan Culpepper, Anatomy of the Fourth Gospel: A Study in Literary Design (Philadelphia: Fortress Press, 1983), chapter on characterization. John 13:23 introduces, without warning or explanation, `one of his disciples, whom Jesus loved,'' reclining close enough to lean back against him at table. No figure answering to this exact description appears anywhere in Mark, Matthew, or Luke; the Beloved Disciple is a distinctly Johannine character who will recur at the cross, the empty tomb, and the epilogue, and whose identity the Gospel itself never states outright. Culpepper reads the figure's anonymity as a deliberate narrative strategy, inviting the reader into the position of ideal disciple rather than settling a historical question.

196 — The traditional answer: John son of Zebedee

Irenaeus of Lyons, Against Heresies, Book 3, chapter 1, section 1 (as preserved in Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History, Book 5, chapter 8); D.A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 1991), introduction and commentary ad loc. John 13:23; 21:20-24. Church tradition from at least the late second century identifies the Beloved Disciple with the apostle John son of Zebedee, author of the Fourth Gospel by the same tradition. Carson defends this identification as still the best-fitting reading of the internal evidence, particularly the Beloved Disciple's consistent proximity to Peter across scenes where the Synoptics place John son of Zebedee alongside Peter as well; the identification remains the majority position in more conservative commentary traditions, though it rests on external patristic testimony rather than anything the Fourth Gospel states by name.

197 — Two live alternatives: Lazarus, or an idealized figure

J.N. Sanders, `Those Whom Jesus Loved (St John xi. 5),'' New Testament Studies 1, no. 1 (1954): 29-41; Floyd V. Filson, `Who Was the Beloved Disciple?'' Journal of Biblical Literature 68, no. 2 (1949): 83-88; compare Rudolf Bultmann, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, trans. G.R. Beasley-Murray (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1971), and Raymond E. Brown, The Community of the Beloved Disciple (New York: Paulist Press, 1979). Sanders and Filson both note that Lazarus is the one named individual the Fourth Gospel explicitly says Jesus loved (John 11:3, 11:5), making him a real candidate for the unnamed disciple introduced here. Bultmann, in a different direction, reads the Beloved Disciple as at least partly an idealized, representative figure of faithful discipleship rather than a straightforwardly historical individual; Brown's later, more cautious book treats the figure's precise identity as an open question the Gospel itself may not intend to answer, a shift from the more traditional leanings of his earlier Anchor Bible commentary volumes. No consensus exists among mainstream scholars on any of these three positions.

198 — How a supper guest ends up leaning on the host

Dennis E. Smith, From Symposium to Eucharist: The Banquet in the Early Christian World (Minneapolis: Fortress Press, 2003), chapters on Greco-Roman and Jewish banquet customs. Diners at a formal first-century meal reclined on their left side on couches or cushions arranged around a low table, propped on the left elbow with feet extended away from the table, which physically allowed the person occupying the position immediately to a host's right to lean back and rest against the host's chest, exactly as the Beloved Disciple does to ask Jesus a question at Peter's signal (John 13:23-25). Smith's study also situates the host's gesture of handing a specially dipped piece of bread to a particular guest, as Jesus does to identify Judas (13:26), as a recognized mark of honor within the same banquet conventions, which sharpens the irony of the same gesture marking out the betrayer.

199 — When did Satan enter Judas? Luke and John disagree on timing

C.K. Barrett, The Gospel According to St John, 2nd ed. (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1978), commentary ad loc. John 13:27; compare Luke 22:3. Luke places Satan's entry into Judas earlier, before the meal, immediately motivating his approach to the chief priests to arrange the betrayal (Luke 22:3-6). John instead places it during the meal itself, at the precise moment Judas takes the dipped morsel from Jesus's hand (John 13:27). Barrett notes the two Gospels agree on the theological claim that satanic influence, not mere greed or disillusionment, drives Judas's act, but disagree on exactly when in the sequence of events that influence takes hold, a small but genuine divergence between two independent tellings of the same betrayal.

200 — And it was night — a plain fact and a loaded one

R. Alan Culpepper, Anatomy of the Fourth Gospel: A Study in Literary Design (Philadelphia: Fortress Press, 1983), chapter on the Gospel's use of light and darkness imagery; compare Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John (XIII-XXI), Anchor Bible vol. 29A (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1970), commentary ad loc. John 13:30. The bare statement that closes Judas's exit, `and it was night,'' works first as ordinary narrative bookkeeping: an evening meal has run late. Culpepper reads it simultaneously as a deliberate return to the Fourth Gospel's recurring light and darkness dualism, in which walking in darkness signals separation from Jesus who is the light of the world (compare John 3:19-21; 9:4-5; 11:9-10); Judas's literal departure into the dark street doubles, in Culpepper's reading, as his narrative departure from the light altogether. The two readings are not competing options requiring a choice between them; John's prose regularly works on both levels at once.

201 — A new commandment, and a narrower one

Wayne A. Meeks, `The Man from Heaven in Johannine Sectarianism,'' Journal of Biblical Literature 91, no. 1 (1972): 44-72. Jesus's command to `love one another as I have loved you'' (John 13:34-35) has no direct Synoptic parallel, and its scope is explicitly directed inward, toward mutual love among Jesus's own disciples, rather than outward toward neighbor or enemy as in the Synoptic love commands drawn from Leviticus 19:18 (compare Mark 12:31; Matthew 5:44; Luke 6:27). Meeks's classic study of Johannine sectarianism reads this in-group focus as characteristic of a community that had experienced real social separation from the wider synagogue and world, for whom mutual love among insiders functioned as both an ethical command and a badge of communal identity.

202 — The Last Supper with no this-is-my-body

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John (XIII-XXI), Anchor Bible vol. 29A (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1970), introduction to the Last Supper narrative, chapters 13-17; D. Moody Smith, John, Abingdon New Testament Commentaries (Nashville: Abingdon Press, 1999), introduction to the Farewell Discourse. All three Synoptic Gospels and Paul's independent tradition in 1 Corinthians 11:23-26 record Jesus identifying the bread and cup as his body and blood at the Last Supper; John's account of the same meal, spanning five chapters, contains no such words at all, replacing them with the foot washing and an extended farewell discourse. Brown and Smith both treat this as one of the most discussed compositional puzzles in Johannine scholarship, precisely because the omission is too large and too consistent across the whole scene to be an oversight; it must reflect a deliberate authorial or editorial choice.

203 — One theory: the reader already has it, from chapter 6

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John (I-XII), Anchor Bible vol. 29 (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1966), commentary on the Bread of Life discourse, John 6:22-59; Urban C. von Wahlde, The Gospel and Letters of John, vol. 2, Eerdmans Critical Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 2010), commentary ad loc. John 6:51-58. One proposed explanation for the missing institution words is that John has already delivered the Eucharistic teaching earlier, in the Bread of Life discourse's language of eating Jesus's flesh and drinking his blood (John 6:51-58), and considered a second, explicit Last Supper version unnecessary for readers who had followed the Gospel that far. Brown and von Wahlde both note this reading fits John's broader habit of scattering material the Synoptics cluster in one scene across the whole Gospel instead.

204 — Another theory: a Last Supper rebuilt as a farewell testament

Fernando F. Segovia, The Farewell of the Word: The Johannine Call to Abide (Minneapolis: Fortress Press, 1991), introduction and chapters 1-2. A second explanation reads John 13-17 as a deliberate reworking of Last Supper material into the literary genre of the Jewish farewell testament — a dying or departing figure's final instructions to followers, a form also visible in texts like the Testament of the Twelve Patriarchs. Segovia argues that on this reading, the foot washing supplies the concrete acted-out lesson a testament genre calls for, and the discourse chapters that follow supply the verbal instruction, so that John is not omitting the institution tradition so much as substituting a different governing genre for the whole scene.

205 — A third theory: a different underlying source

Rudolf Bultmann, The Gospel of John: A Commentary, trans. G.R. Beasley-Murray (Philadelphia: Westminster Press, 1971), introduction on sources; Urban C. von Wahlde, The Gospel and Letters of John, vol. 1, Eerdmans Critical Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 2010), introduction on compositional history. A third proposed explanation is source-critical rather than literary: Bultmann's reconstruction of the Fourth Gospel's sources posits a Passion tradition distinct from and largely independent of the Synoptic passion source, one that may simply never have carried an institution-of-the-Eucharist unit at all. Von Wahlde's multi-edition theory of John's composition similarly allows that the version of the Last Supper preserved in the Fourth Gospel's earliest layer drew on different underlying material than Mark's, rather than the author of John having deliberately deleted a tradition he otherwise knew.

206 — A prediction all four Gospels place at the same supper

Raymond E. Brown, The Death of the Messiah: From Gethsemane to the Grave, vol. 1, Anchor Bible Reference Library (New York: Doubleday, 1994), commentary on the denial predictions. Jesus's prediction that Peter will deny him three times before the rooster crows appears in Mark 14:29-31, Matthew 26:33-35, Luke 22:33-34, and John 13:36-38, in each case placed at the Last Supper on the same night as the arrest that follows. Brown's exhaustive Passion-narrative commentary treats the prediction's consistent placement across all four independent tellings as a point of broad agreement in the tradition, unlike many other Johannine details in this chapter that diverge sharply from the Synoptic sequence; the location and substance of this particular prediction is not a distinctly Johannine peculiarity.

207 — A dying figure's last instructions: the farewell-discourse form

George R. Beasley-Murray, John, Word Biblical Commentary vol. 36, 2nd ed. (Nashville: Thomas Nelson, 1999), introductory discussion preceding John 13-17. Beasley-Murray situates John 13:31-16:33 within the well-attested ancient literary genre of the farewell discourse or testament, a departing figure's final address to followers, combining predictions of the speaker's departure, moral exhortation, comfort, and promises for the future, and compares it to Jacob's deathbed address to his sons (Genesis 49) and Moses's farewell addresses in Deuteronomy 31-33, alongside extra-biblical Jewish testament literature (such as the Testaments of the Twelve Patriarchs); on this reading the discourse's shape is a recognized compositional form rather than a stenographic transcript of one evening's table talk.

208 — Two discourses spliced into one: Brown on the doubling in John 13-17

Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John XIII-XXI, Anchor Bible vol. 29A (Garden City, NY: Doubleday & Company, 1970), Introduction to the Book of Glory, section on the Last Discourse. Brown argues the material now printed as one continuous farewell discourse (John 13:31-17:26) shows signs of being composed from at least two originally separate discourses on similar themes, joined by a Johannine editor, with John 14 forming a complete, self-contained unit that already covers departure, the Father's house, the Paraclete, and peace before chapters 15-16 largely repeat the same themes in different language.

209 — Mansions or rooms? A translation history

D.A. Carson, The Gospel According to John, Pillar New Testament Commentary (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 1991), commentary ad loc. John 14:2.
