The river in which John the Baptist conducts his ministry, and the setting for this chapter's baptismal scenes (1:28, 1:29-34) — a real, geographically uncontroversial river, though the precise ancient location of "Bethany beyond the Jordan" on its banks is not (see BETHANY BEYOND THE JORDAN).

Cited in: John 1:28, 1:29-34.

JOSEPH OF ARIMATHEA

A wealthy member of the Sanhedrin and, per John, a secret disciple of Jesus `for fear of the Jews,'' who requests Jesus's body from Pilate after the crucifixion and provides the tomb for his burial (19:38). He appears independently in all four Gospels, a pattern of multiple attestation scholars weigh as evidence he is more plausibly a remembered historical figure than an invented one [313].

JUDAS (NOT ISCARIOT)

A member of the Twelve who asks Jesus, at the Last Supper, why he will manifest himself to the disciples and not to the world (14:22), his only appearance anywhere in the New Testament with a speaking part or individual description, identified solely by the negative disambiguation distinguishing him from Judas Iscariot. Whether he should be equated with the Judas son of James of Luke 6:16/Acts 1:13 or the Thaddaeus of Mark 3:18/Matthew 10:3 is a genuine, unresolved harmonization question, since the Synoptic apostle lists disagree with each other on the name in that list position [220] [221].

JUDAS ISCARIOT

One of the Twelve, identified at his first appearance in John's Gospel as the coming betrayer and, in Jesus's own words, `a devil'' (6:70-71). He is named here as the son of Simon Iscariot, though the manuscript tradition itself is divided over whether `Iscariot'' names a family line or, in an older sense, marks Judas (or his father) as a `man of Kerioth'' [96].

KIDRON VALLEY

The valley east of Jerusalem's Temple Mount that Jesus and his disciples cross to reach the garden where he is arrested (18:1). John does not name the garden itself, unlike the Synoptics' Gethsemane, locating it only by this crossing.

LAZARUS OF BETHANY

The brother of Martha and Mary of Bethany, introduced in this chapter as gravely ill (11:1-3) and later raised from death after four days in the tomb (11:38-44), the climactic sign of the first half of John's Gospel. His story appears nowhere in Mark, Matthew, or Luke, a genuine and much-discussed gap in the tradition [158] [159]; the only other Lazarus named in the canonical Gospels is the beggar of Jesus's parable in Luke 16:19-31, a coincidence some scholars read as more than coincidental [160]. He returns as a living witness to Jesus's power in the following chapter's dinner at Bethany (12:1-11), where the chief priests are reported plotting to kill him too, precisely because his continued presence keeps drawing believers.

LINEN WRAPPINGS AND FACE-CLOTH (JOHN 20:6-7)

The grave clothes Peter and the Beloved Disciple find inside the empty tomb (20:6-7): linen wrappings (othonia) and a separate face-cloth (soudarion), the latter 'rolled up in a place by itself.' Unique to John among the Gospels, the detail's orderly arrangement functions within the narrative as an implicit argument against the possibility of hasty grave-robbery [321].

MAKING HIMSELF EQUAL WITH GOD (JOHN 5:18)

The charge leveled against Jesus after he defends healing on the Sabbath by saying, My Father is working still, and I am working (5:17), a claim the narrative says intensified the authorities' hostility, since calling God his own Father was heard as making himself God's equal. The discourse that follows immediately qualifies rather than embraces that reading, presenting the Son's authority as entirely derived from and dependent on the Father [70] [71].

MALCHUS

The high priest's servant whose right ear Peter cuts off with a sword during the arrest in the garden (18:10); Jesus heals the wound in Luke's parallel account (Luke 22:51), though John does not record a healing. Only John names both the servant and the disciple who strikes him; the Synoptics leave both anonymous [292].

MANNA (BREAD FROM HEAVEN)

The miraculous wilderness food described in Exodus 16, cited by the crowd (6:31, quoting a composite drawn from Exodus 16:4 and Psalm 78:24) as the sign-precedent they expect Jesus to match or exceed. Jesus's answer reroutes the tradition, insisting the true bread from heaven was never the manna itself but what it pointed toward [98].

MARTHA

A resident of Bethany, sister of Mary and Lazarus, introduced here confronting Jesus on the road into the village — "Lord, if you had been here, my brother would not have died" — and then offering, in reply to his declaration "I am the resurrection and the life," one of the Gospel's clearest full confessions of faith: "I believe that you are the Christ, the Son of God, who is coming into the world" (11:21-27) [155]. She reappears serving the meal at the following chapter's Bethany dinner (12:2).

MARY MAGDALENE

The first person, in all four canonical Gospels, to encounter either the empty tomb or the risen Jesus; in John specifically, she is the first to find the stone removed (20:1), the first to see and speak with the risen Jesus, mistaking him for the gardener until he says her name (20:14-16), and the first to proclaim, 'I have seen the Lord' (20:18). All four Gospels agree on her priority despite disagreeing on many surrounding details [317] [324], a point of some evidentiary weight given the reduced legal standing of women's testimony in the period [325]. She is also named among the women standing at the foot of the cross in the preceding chapter (19:25), her presence there attested independently across all four canonical Passion narratives.

MARY OF BETHANY

Sister of Martha and Lazarus, identified by the narrator on her first mention here as the same Mary "who anointed the Lord with ointment and wiped his feet with her hair" (11:2) — a scene John has not yet told and narrates only in the following chapter (12:1-8), a detail commentators take as evidence the evangelist assumed his audience already knew her story. In this chapter she stays seated at home until summoned, then falls at Jesus's feet with the same words Martha used and weeps openly among the gathered mourners (11:28-33).

MARY, WIFE OF CLOPAS (THE THREE-OR-FOUR-WOMEN QUESTION)

Named in John 19:25 in a list, `his mother, and his mother's sister, Mary the wife of Clopas, and Mary Magdalene,'' whose Greek grammar does not make clear whether `his mother's sister'' and `Mary the wife of Clopas'' name one woman or two, leaving genuine, unresolved scholarly disagreement over whether three or four women stood at the cross [306]. Clopas himself appears by name only here and, in a debated connection, at Luke 24:18, where a disciple named Cleopas meets the risen Jesus on the Emmaus road.

MICAH (PROPHET)

An eighth-century BCE Judean prophet whose oracle naming Bethlehem as the origin of a future ruler (Micah 5:2) supplies the scriptural objection some in the Tabernacles crowd raise against Jesus's messianic claim, since he is known publicly as a Galilean (7:41-42) [107] [106].

MONE (ROOMS/DWELLING PLACES, JOHN 14:2)

The Greek noun behind the King James Version's rendering many mansions, in John 14:2, more accurately translated rooms or dwelling places, an ordinary lodging-place, not a grand estate; the KJV's word choice descends from the Latin Vulgate's mansiones, itself a term for a traveler's overnight way-station [209]. The same root recurs at 14:23, where Jesus promises that he and the Father will make their mone, their home, with the one who loves him, a verbal echo largely invisible once the two verses are translated with unrelated English words [210].

MOSES (ACCUSER AND WITNESS)

Israel's lawgiver, invoked twice in this chapter's discourse: first as part of the underlying Deuteronomic tradition of a future prophet like Moses that shapes the chapter's christological claims, and finally by name as a witness who will himself accuse Jesus's hearers, since he wrote of me (5:45-47) [73].

MOUNT GERIZIM

The Samaritans' holy mountain overlooking Shechem/Nablus, named by the woman at the well as the place "our fathers worshiped" (4:20) in contrast to Jerusalem. Excavation has confirmed a genuine Samaritan temple and city occupied the summit from the Persian through Hellenistic periods [36], until the Hasmonean ruler John Hyrcanus I destroyed it roughly a century and a half before this scene [37]. The Samaritan Pentateuch's own text of Deuteronomy 27:4 names this mountain, not Ebal, as the place Israel was commanded to build its altar [38].

MYRRH AND ALOES (BURIAL SPICES)

The burial preparation Nicodemus brings, roughly seventy-five Roman pounds, about a hundred English pounds, of myrrh mixed with aloes, used to pack the linen wrappings around Jesus's body per Jewish burial custom (19:39-40). Commentators widely read the sheer quantity, comparable to the spices marking King Asa's royal burial in 2 Chronicles 16:14, as a deliberate signal of honor rather than a routine, realistic burial detail [314].

N. T. WRIGHT

British New Testament scholar and bishop whose multi-volume Christian Origins and the Question of God project culminates in a lengthy historical argument that the specific, combined shape of the empty-tomb and appearance traditions — including the encounters narrated across John 20 — is best explained by something genuinely anomalous having occurred, against more skeptical visionary-experience readings of the same evidence [320].

NATHANAEL

Recruited by Philip, and initially skeptical — "Can anything good come out of Nazareth?" — until Jesus tells him he saw him "under the fig tree" before Philip ever called him, at which point Nathanael declares him "the Son of God" and "the King of Israel" without further argument (1:45-49). Nathanael appears only in John's Gospel, here and again at a resurrection appearance in chapter 21 (21:2), and never in Matthew's, Mark's, or Luke's lists of the Twelve apostles. Whether Nathanael is the same person the Synoptics and Acts call "Bartholomew" — always paired with Philip in their own apostle lists, exactly as John pairs Philip with Nathanael — is a genuinely open scholarly question, not a settled identification (see Note 14).

Cited in: John 1:45-51; see Note 14.

NAZARETH

Nathanael's dismissive question — "Can anything good come out of Nazareth?" (1:46) — is itself a small, checkable data point: Nazareth was a small, obscure Galilean village, unmentioned anywhere in the Hebrew Bible, the Talmud, or Josephus's extensive first-century writings, consistent with Nathanael's low opinion of the place having some basis in its actual, unremarkable size and reputation rather than being invented purely for dramatic effect.

Cited in: John 1:45-46.

NAZARETH INSCRIPTION

A marble slab inscribed in Greek with an imperial edict — headed diatagma Kaisaros, ordinance of Caesar — forbidding the disturbance of tombs and prescribing a capital charge for removing a buried body. Bought in 1878 by the antiquarian Wilhelm Froehner with a note saying only that it came from Nazareth, and never excavated. For most of the twentieth century it was offered as documentary corroboration of the empty-tomb controversy. In 2020 stable-isotope analysis matched its marble to a quarry on the Greek island of Kos, removing the basis for the Nazareth attribution and suggesting a different occasion entirely: the desecrated tomb of Nikias, a tyrant of Kos. Still a real Roman edict about grave-robbing; no longer evidence about this story.

NEW COMMANDMENT (JOHN 13:34-35)

Jesus's instruction, given immediately after Judas's departure, that his disciples love one another as he has loved them, and that this mutual love is how everyone will recognize them as his disciples (John 13:34-35). The command has no direct Synoptic parallel and is explicitly directed inward, toward the community of Jesus's own followers, in contrast to the Synoptic love commands aimed outward toward neighbor and enemy [201].

NICODEMUS

A Pharisee and "ruler of the Jews" — this Gospel's own shorthand for a seat on the Sanhedrin (see SANHEDRIN) — who comes to Jesus at night in this chapter, is confused by Jesus's teaching that one must be born anōthen, and reappears twice more in this Gospel: defending Jesus's right to a hearing before his fellow rulers (7:50-51) and bringing seventy-five pounds of myrrh and aloes for Jesus's burial after the crucifixion (19:39-42). He is named nowhere else in the New Testament — not in Matthew, Mark, or Luke, in any form. A nineteenth-century proposal identified him with Nicodemus (Naqdimon) ben Gurion, a wealthy Jerusalemite the Talmud remembers for wonder-working generosity toward the city during the siege of the First Jewish-Roman War (b. Ta'anit 19b-20a; b. Gittin 56a) — an identification most modern scholarship rejects on chronological grounds, since the Talmudic figure belongs to the 60s CE, roughly forty years after Nicodemus's recorded conversation with Jesus [24]. Whether he is a real, minor historical figure the other three Gospels simply had no reason to include, or a deliberate Johannine literary construction standing in for the "many rulers" this Gospel says secretly believed in Jesus but "did not confess it" for fear of the Pharisees (12:42), remains a genuinely open scholarly question [24].

Cited in: John 3:1-21, 7:50-51, 19:39-42; see Note 24.

ORIGINAL ENDING OF JOHN (JOHN 20:30-31)

The Gospel's explicit statement of purpose — that 'these are written so that you may believe... and that by believing you may have life in his name' — read by a broad, though not absolutely unanimous, body of scholarship as the book's originally intended conclusion [332] [334] [333], with chapter 21 understood as a later appended epilogue. No surviving manuscript preserves the Gospel without chapter 21, so the judgment remains a critical inference rather than something the manuscript tradition itself directly attests.

OTHER SHEEP NOT OF THIS FOLD

Jesus's statement in John 10:16 that he has sheep beyond his present hearers, whom he must also bring in, so that there will be one flock and one shepherd. Mainstream historical-critical scholarship widely reads this as a retrospective reference to the Gentile mission of the early church, composed and placed in Jesus's mouth after that mission was already underway, rather than a plan the historical Jesus is likely to have articulated in these terms during his lifetime [137] [138].

PAPIAS OF HIERAPOLIS

An early second-century bishop of Hierapolis in Asia Minor whose now-lost writings are known chiefly through quotations preserved by the fourth-century historian Eusebius of Caesarea. Eusebius reports that Papias related a story about a woman accused of many sins before the Lord, cited in this chapter as early, independent evidence that some version of the pericope adulterae tradition circulated in Christian memory long before it entered any surviving manuscript of John [53].

PARACLETE (HOLY SPIRIT)

The term (Greek parakletos) Jesus uses for the Spirit he promises the Father will send (14:16-17, 14:26), rendered variously across English translations as Advocate, Helper, Comforter, or Counselor, no single English word captures both the term's legal and forensic sense, one called alongside to speak in a person's defense, and its broader sense of a supportive presence summoned to aid someone, a genuine ambiguity in the underlying Greek rather than a translation shortfall [212]. The term's precise conceptual background remains debated: Rudolf Bultmann proposed a pre-Christian Gnostic redeemer-myth origin [213], while Otto Betz argued for a Jewish legal-advocate and intercessor background [214]; neither position has become consensus. The Greek allon, another, in the phrase another Helper specifies another of the same kind, implying Jesus has already been functioning as a parakletos for the disciples [215]. This is the Paraclete's first appearance in the Gospel; the term recurs in John 15-16.

PASSOVER

One of three pilgrimage festivals (with Shavuot/Pentecost and Sukkot/Tabernacles) that observant Jews across the Mediterranean world were expected to mark by traveling to Jerusalem, commemorating the Exodus from Egypt. The festival swelled Jerusalem's population from an ordinary size of roughly 55,000 to several times that, by modern scholarly estimate — far below the millions Josephus himself claimed, a figure historians generally treat as exaggeration [22]. Both the temple incident of John 2:13-22 and the closing summary of 2:23-25 are set during this same Passover.

PASSOVER AMNESTY CUSTOM (UNATTESTED OUTSIDE THE GOSPELS)

The alleged practice, invoked by Pilate here (18:39), of a Roman governor releasing one prisoner chosen by the crowd at Passover. No independent first-century source, Jewish or Roman, confirms any such custom, making its historicity a genuinely open scholarly question rather than an established fact [289].

PERICOPE ADULTERAE

The Latin technical name (literally "the passage of the adulteress") scholars use for the story of the woman caught in adultery in John 7:53–8:11, treated in this chapter as a case study in New Testament textual criticism [46] [50]. The near-universal critical judgment is that it was not part of John's Gospel as originally composed, a conclusion about manuscript transmission that leaves the story's underlying historicity a separate, open question [52].

PETER'S DEATH PREDICTION (JOHN 21:18-19)

Jesus's cryptic words to Peter about being dressed and carried where he does not wish to go in his old age, explained by the narrator as indicating 'by what death he was to glorify God' (21:19). Read by close to the full range of scholarship as a prediction composed after its own fulfillment, since Peter's actual death was already known to the Johannine community by the time this chapter was written [347]. Later tradition — Tertullian, then Origen via Eusebius, then the apocryphal Acts of Peter — supplies the specific detail of an inverted crucifixion at Rome that the Gospel text itself never states [348] [349] [350].

PHILIP (APOSTLE)

Found directly by Jesus the day after Andrew and Simon Peter join him, and immediately recruits Nathanael with the words "We have found him about whom Moses in the law and also the prophets wrote" (1:43-45). John's Gospel consistently pairs Philip with Nathanael; the Synoptic Gospels and Acts instead pair Philip immediately with a "Bartholomew" in all three apostle lists, a naming puzzle discussed at NATHANAEL below.

Cited in: John 1:43-46; see Note 14.

PHILO OF ALEXANDRIA

A Hellenistic Jewish philosopher active in Alexandria, Egypt, roughly 20 BCE to 50 CE — an almost exact contemporary of the apostolic generation — whose surviving Greek treatises develop an extensive Logos doctrine describing the Logos as God's mediating "eldest son" and, in one passage, even a "second god," loosely speaking. Scholars are divided over how directly, if at all, John's own prologue draws on Philo's specific writings versus a broader shared Hellenistic Jewish tradition (see Notes 2, 4, 5).

Cited in: background to John 1:1-18 (not itself named in the text); see Notes 2, 4, 5.

PONTIUS PILATE

Roman prefect of Judea (26-36 CE) who presides over Jesus's trial in this chapter, repeatedly declaring `I find no guilt in him'' (18:38; 19:4, 6) while ultimately handing him over for crucifixion under pressure from the chief priests and the crowd's `not Caesar's friend'' threat (19:12). Non-Christian sources (Josephus, Philo) attest Pilate as a real historical prefect; John's portrait of him as unusually scrupulous and reluctant is weighed by scholars against a blunter, more forceful Pilate in those independent sources [296]. Judea's Roman governors normally resided at Caesarea Maritima on the coast, coming to Jerusalem chiefly for festival crowds such as this one [287].

POOL OF SILOAM

A spring-fed pool in the southeastern part of first-century Jerusalem, the water source drawn from each morning of the Feast of Tabernacles for the water-libation ceremony this chapter's living water saying presupposes (7:37-39) [108]. The same pool later serves as the site of a healing in John 9.

PRAETORIUM (PILATE'S HEADQUARTERS)

Herod the Great's former palace in Jerusalem, used as the Roman governor's residence and headquarters during his visits to the city, where Jesus's trial before Pilate takes place (18:28-38). The Jewish leaders themselves refuse to enter it, to avoid ritual defilement before eating the Passover meal [282], so Pilate conducts much of the exchange by stepping outside to them.

PSALM 82 ("I SAID, YOU ARE GODS")

The psalm Jesus cites in his own defense after being accused of blasphemy for making himself God (10:33-34), quoting Psalm 82:6's address to 'gods' whose own judgment is condemned later in the same psalm. Jesus's use of the citation follows a recognized rabbinic a fortiori argument form [144]. The psalm's own original referent for 'gods' is itself a live scholarly dispute: the historical-critical mainstream reads it as human judges or rulers addressed with an honorific divine title [145], while a substantial minority reading takes it as literal members of a divine council [146].

RECLINING AT TABLE (BANQUET CUSTOM)

The Greco-Roman and Jewish practice of dining while reclined on the left side on a couch or cushions around a low table, feet extended away from it. The custom explains how the disciple whom Jesus loved could physically lean back against Jesus's chest to ask a whispered question at Peter's signal (John 13:23-25), and frames the honor implied by Jesus handing a specially dipped morsel of bread to a particular guest (13:26) [198].

RITUAL PURITY BEFORE PASSOVER (JOHN 18:28)

The stated reason the Jewish leaders will not enter the Praetorium: entering a Gentile residence would render them ritually unclean and unable to eat the Passover meal. The detail creates one of the most substantive, honestly disputed chronological tensions between John and the Synoptics, since it implies the priests in this scene have not yet eaten the Passover meal on the very morning of Jesus's trial, in contrast to the Synoptics' explicit Passover Last Supper the night before [282].

RIVERS OF LIVING WATER (JOHN 7:37-39)

Jesus's declaration on the festival's last day that out of his heart will flow rivers of living water, identified by the narrator as a reference to the Spirit believers would later receive, since the Spirit had not yet been given because Jesus was not yet glorified (7:39). The saying is anchored in the concrete water-libation ceremony of the feast rather than a free-floating metaphor [108], and its exact grammatical subject, the believer's heart or Jesus's own, remains a genuine, debated crux [110].

ROLLING-STONE TOMBS (FIRST-CENTURY JERUSALEM)

The large disc-shaped blocking stone depicted sealing Jesus's tomb was a genuinely attested but archaeologically uncommon installation in first-century Jerusalem-area burial; most excavated tombs from the period instead used smaller square or rectangular blocking stones [322], set within a broader excavated tomb typology of kokh niches and arcosolia recesses [323].

ROYAL OFFICIAL (CAPERNAUM)

An unnamed officer of Herod Antipas's court (Greek basilikos, "royal" or "belonging to the king") whose son lies near death at Capernaum. He travels to Cana to beg Jesus for healing, and his son recovers at the exact hour Jesus pronounces him well, without Jesus ever traveling to see him (4:46-54). His court rank, rather than any Roman military office, is the clearest textual distinction between his story and the Synoptic centurion whose servant Jesus heals in a similar way [43] [44].

SABBATH (SECOND TEMPLE LAW)

The seventh-day rest commanded in the Decalogue, elaborated by the first century into a body of case law about which activities counted as forbidden work. The man's carrying of his mat, and Jesus's act of healing itself, both become flashpoints in this chapter precisely because Sabbath boundaries were a live, contested area of Jewish legal reasoning rather than a single settled rule [68] [69].

SAMARIA

The central hill-country region between Galilee and Judea, home to the Samaritan community and the territory Jesus and his disciples pass through on their return to Galilee (4:4). Its own sanctuary tradition, centered on Mount Gerizim, stood in longstanding rivalry with Jerusalem's Temple claim [35].

SAMARITAN WOMAN AT THE WELL

An unnamed Samaritan woman who meets Jesus at Jacob's well, engages him in an extended dialogue about living water and worship, and becomes one of the first people in John's Gospel to hear Jesus openly identify himself as the Messiah (4:26). The text never gives her a name; that detail comes only from later, non-canonical tradition. Her five husbands have been read either as a piece of personal biography or as an allegory for Samaria's mixed religious history under 2 Kings 17, a debate the text itself does not resolve [40].

SAMARITANS

The ethnic-religious community, centered on Shechem/Nablus and Mount Gerizim, that the woman at the well and the townspeople of Sychar belong to in this chapter. Their split from Jerusalem-centered Judaism has roots reaching back to the Assyrian resettlement of the northern kingdom in 722 BCE and hardened over the Persian and Hellenistic periods [35]. John's aside that Jews and Samaritans have no dealings with one another (4:9) reflects real social distance and periodic violence but overstates a uniform, total separation against the fuller archaeological and Josephan record [33].

SANHEDRIN

Judea's highest native Jewish council under Roman rule, the body this Gospel means when it calls Nicodemus "a ruler of the Jews" (3:1) and the same council that later convenes to discuss Jesus (11:47) and holds him before Caiaphas (18:12-14). Its existence is independently attested: Josephus records the Roman governor Aulus Gabinius dividing Judea into five districts, each under its own synedrion (council), as early as 57 BCE [23]. What is genuinely unsettled is how closely its actual first-century composition and powers under Roman prefectural rule matched the detailed seventy-one-member "Great Sanhedrin," with its graduated system of lesser twenty-three-member courts, that the Mishnah describes generations later, compiled around 200 CE — long after Rome had already dissolved the historical body along with the Temple in 70 CE [23]. Historians broadly accept that some form of native ruling council mattered under Rome; how much of the Mishnah's orderly, idealized picture reflects the institution Nicodemus and Jesus's own judges actually sat on, rather than a later rabbinic reconstruction, is an open historiographical question.

Cited in: John 3:1; see Note 23.

SATAN (ENTERS JUDAS, JOHN 13:27)

The adversarial figure John says enters Judas Iscariot at the moment he takes the dipped morsel from Jesus's hand (John 13:27), prompting Jesus's command that he do quickly what he intends to do. Luke's Gospel places the same satanic entry earlier, before the meal (Luke 22:3), a small but genuine divergence in timing between two independent tellings of the same betrayal [199].

SEA OF GALILEE (SEA OF TIBERIAS)

The freshwater lake in Galilee that Jesus and the crowd cross in this chapter (6:1, 6:16-21), called by John both `the Sea of Galilee'' and `the Sea of Tiberias'' — the second name reflecting the real first-century renaming of the region after the lakeside city Herod Antipas founded and named for the emperor Tiberius [90].

SEAMLESS TUNIC AND CASTING LOTS (PSALM 22:18)

The soldiers divide Jesus's outer garments among themselves but cast lots for his one-piece, seamless undertunic rather than tear it (19:23-24), an act John reads as a direct fulfillment of Psalm 22:18, `they divide my garments among them, and for my clothing they cast lots.'' Psalm 22 supplies concrete details across all four Gospels' Passion narratives, a pattern of scriptural shaping shared with the Synoptics rather than unique to John [305].

SEVEN-SCENE STRUCTURE OF JOHN 9

Raymond E. Brown's literary observation that the healing of the man born blind is composed as a tightly constructed drama unfolding across seven scenes — Jesus and the disciples, the healing itself, the neighbors' dispute, the first Pharisaic interrogation, the parents' testimony, the second interrogation and expulsion, and Jesus's return to the man — with the man's own testimony escalating in confidence at each turn while the authorities' position hardens in the opposite direction [130].

SIGNS SOURCE (JOHANNINE)

A hypothetical earlier document, proposed by source critics including Rudolf Bultmann and reconstructed at length by Robert T. Fortna, imagined as an underlying miracle-list that John's author incorporated into the finished Gospel. The theory rests substantially on John's own explicit numbering of its miracles: the Cana wine miracle as "the first of his signs" (2:11) and this chapter's Capernaum healing as "the second sign" (4:54) [45].

SIMON PETER (CEPHAS)

Brought to Jesus by his brother Andrew and renamed on sight: "You are Simon son of John; you are to be called Cephas" (which is translated Peter) (1:42). "Cephas" transliterates the Aramaic Kepha, "rock"; "Peter" translates the same meaning into Greek (Petros) — not two different nicknames but the same one in two languages, independently confirmed decades before any Gospel by Paul's own letters, which use both forms interchangeably within a single letter (see Note 12). A genuine minority scholarly theory once argued Cephas and Peter were originally two different people later merged by scribal tradition; it has since been abandoned by virtually every scholar who has held it, including its own original proponent (see Note 13).

Cited in: John 1:40-42; see Notes 12, 13.

SOLOMON'S PORTICO

A covered colonnade along the eastern edge of the Jerusalem Temple platform, where Jesus is walking when confronted about his identity in this chapter's second scene (10:23). Josephus independently describes this portico as a surviving remnant of Solomon's original Temple construction, giving John's setting detail a concrete, externally attested architectural referent [143].

SON OF DESTRUCTION (HO HUIOS TES APOLEIAS)

A rare title applied without naming him to Judas Iscariot at John 17:12, describing the one disciple lost `that the Scripture might be fulfilled.'' Its only other New Testament occurrence is 2 Thessalonians 2:3, describing the eschatological `man of lawlessness'' — a striking verbal parallel across two otherwise unrelated texts, not evidence of direct literary dependence [263]. Commentators connect the verse back to the Psalm 41:9 citation applied to Judas's betrayal in John 13:18 [264].

SONG OF THE VINEYARD (ISAIAH 5:1-7)

A short poem in Isaiah, structured as a love song for a friend's vineyard that curdles into a courtroom indictment once the vineyard yields only wild grapes; its final lines name the vineyard outright as "the house of Israel" [226]. It stands as one of the most fully developed instances of the broader vine-as-Israel tradition John 15 draws on [224].

SPEIRA (ROMAN MILITARY DETACHMENT)

The Greek term John uses for the armed body accompanying Judas at the arrest (18:3), elastic enough in contemporary usage to mean anything from a small maniple of a few dozen soldiers up to a full cohort of several hundred. Its presence alongside the Temple police raises a genuine historical-plausibility question about whether Rome would commit troops to arrest one Galilean teacher before any formal charge existed [273], though a Roman garrison cohort is independently attested at the nearby Antonia Fortress, reinforced during festival crowds [274].

STONING (LEVITICUS 24:16 BLASPHEMY PENALTY)

The capital penalty Leviticus 24:16 prescribes for blaspheming God's Name, cited by mainstream commentary as the legal backdrop for both the accusers' demand in 8:3-5 and the crowd's attempt to stone Jesus after "before Abraham was, I am" (8:58-59), where a claim understood as self-identification with the divine Name triggers exactly the response the statute anticipates [62].

SYCHAR

The Samaritan town near Jacob's well where this chapter's events unfold (4:5), home to the townspeople who come to believe in Jesus after the woman's testimony (4:39-42). Whether Sychar is simply another name for the largely abandoned site of ancient Shechem or the separate, still-inhabited village of 'Askar nearby is not settled by the available evidence [30].

TEMPLE OFFICERS (HYPERETAI)

Members of the Temple's Levitical guard, dispatched twice in this chapter by the chief priests and Pharisees to arrest Jesus (7:32, 45) and returning both times without him, the second time reporting only that no one ever spoke like this man [111].

TERTULLIAN

North African Christian writer (c. 155-220 CE), among the earliest sources to connect Peter's martyrdom at Rome to a passion resembling his Lord's, a reading commentators take as drawing on John 21:18's prediction, though writing more than a century after the events he describes [348].

TETELESTAI (``IT IS FINISHED')

Jesus's final recorded word from the cross in John (19:30), a single Greek verb, tetelestai, that can carry both a plain sense of completion and a documented commercial sense, attested in papyri, of a debt marked fully paid, a linguistic double meaning some commentators weigh into the line's theological resonance and others regard as reading too much into an otherwise ordinary word [307].

THE ANGEL AT THE POOL (JOHN 5:3B-4)

A textual passage, present in many later manuscripts and traditional English translations, describing an angel who periodically stirred the pool's water so that the first person into it afterward was healed. The clause is absent from the earliest and best-attested Greek manuscripts and is treated by the critical consensus as a later scribal explanation added to make sense of the healed man's remark in 5:7; modern critical translations bracket, footnote, or omit it [66].

THE BELOVED DISCIPLE (DISCIPLE WHOM JESUS LOVED)

An unnamed disciple, introduced for the first time in this chapter as reclining close enough to Jesus to lean back against him (John 13:23), who recurs at the cross, the empty tomb, and the Gospel's epilogue, and whose identity the Fourth Gospel never states outright [195]. Church tradition from at least the late second century identifies him with the apostle John son of Zebedee [196], while modern scholarship has also proposed Lazarus of Bethany, the one individual John explicitly says Jesus loved (11:3, 11:5), or an idealized, representative figure of ideal discipleship rather than a single historical individual [197].

THE BOY WITH THE LOAVES AND FISH

An unnamed boy in the crowd, pointed out by Andrew, whose five barley loaves and two fish (6:9) become the material for the feeding miracle. Barley marks the loaves as the cheaper grain of the ordinary poor rather than a wealthier household's wheat bread [97], and the boy's provision is read as recalling the Elisha feeding story's own emphasis on a small, seemingly insufficient offering [83].

THE GARDEN TOMB (JOHN 19:41-42)

The `new tomb, in which no one had yet been laid,'' located in a garden near the crucifixion site, where Joseph and Nicodemus lay Jesus's body because of the nearness of the Jewish day of preparation (19:41-42). Rock-cut chamber tombs of exactly this kind, along with linen wrapping and spice-packing, are well attested by archaeology of Second Temple-period Jerusalem, a point of general scholarly agreement about the burial account's basic historical plausibility, distinct from the separate and far more contested question of the resurrection appearances that follow [315].

THE GATE FOR THE SHEEP

A second self-identification Jesus makes within the same discourse (10:7, 9), distinct from, and on a strictly literal reading in some tension with, his claim moments later to be the shepherd who enters by that gate (10:2, 11) [136]. Commentators read the shift either as evidence of two combined source-units [134] or as intentional rhetorical layering characteristic of John's style elsewhere in the Gospel [135].

THE JEWS (HOI IOUDAIOI) IN JOHN

John's recurring phrase the Jews, Greek hoi Ioudaioi, used here of the crowd's fear of speaking openly about Jesus (7:13), most often designates the Jerusalem-based religious authorities hostile to him rather than the Jewish people as a whole, a distinction the commentary tradition treats as important context for reading the term accurately across the Gospel [101].

THE JOHANNINE DISPLACEMENT THEORY (JOHN 5-6)

A source-critical proposal, associated above all with Rudolf Bultmann, that the Fourth Gospel's underlying material has been rearranged from a more geographically coherent original order: chapter 4 ends in Galilee, chapter 5 relocates without explanation to Jerusalem, and chapter 6 resumes in Galilee as though chapter 5 had never happened. Raymond E. Brown and other commentators accept the seam as real while doubting any specific reconstruction can be proven, since no manuscript preserves an alternate sequence [78] [79] [80].

THE JOHANNINE DISPLACEMENT THEORY, JOHN 14:31 SEAM

John 14:31's closing command, Rise, let us go from here, followed immediately by two further chapters of uninterrupted discourse in chapters 15-16 before the group actually departs in chapter 18, is widely regarded as the clearest internal seam in the entire Farewell Discourse, cross-referencing the displacement theory first introduced at the John 5-6 seam (see THE JOHANNINE DISPLACEMENT THEORY (JOHN 5-6)) [222]. Rudolf Bultmann proposed the seam reflects an actual physical disordering of pages in an early manuscript [223]; the theory remains a minority position but is seriously engaged in the commentary tradition, alongside readings of the line as a deliberate authorial pause or a later, imperfectly integrated addition.

THE MAN BORN BLIND

The unnamed central figure of this chapter (9:1-41), blind from birth and healed by Jesus with mud made from spittle and dust, then sent to wash in the Pool of Siloam (9:6-7). His testimony grows steadily bolder across the chapter's escalating interrogation scenes [130] — from a beggar merely defending his own identity ("I am the man," 9:9) to a confident theological arguer ("if this man were not from God, he could do nothing," 9:33) to, finally, a worshiper ("Lord, I believe," 9:38). Like the Samaritan woman at the well and the woman caught in adultery elsewhere in John, he is never given a name.

THE PALATIAL MANSION (ARCHAEOLOGICAL SITE)

A large, lavishly appointed Herodian-period residence excavated in Jerusalem's Upper City Jewish Quarter, among several opulent houses of the same district plausibly connected to the priestly aristocracy of Jesus's day. Cited here as archaeological texture for the kind of high priestly household implied by Annas's and Caiaphas's residences in this chapter, though no excavated structure is inscribed with either man's name [277].

THE POOL OF BETHESDA

The five-porticoed pool complex near Jerusalem's Sheep Gate where Jesus heals a man ill for thirty-eight years (5:1-9). Nineteenth- and twentieth-century excavation beside the Church of St. Anne uncovered a genuine twin-pool structure matching John's architectural description in unusual detail, vindicating a feature earlier critics had assumed was symbolic invention [64] [65].

THE SICK MAN AT BETHESDA

An unnamed man, ill for thirty-eight years, healed by Jesus at the pool on a Sabbath (5:5-9); the text never gives his name or the specific nature of his condition beyond an inability to reach the water unaided. He reports Jesus to the Jewish authorities after being confronted for carrying his mat on the Sabbath, setting the chapter's controversy in motion [68] [69].

THE SPIRIT'S TESTIMONY (JOHN 15:26-27)

The chapter's closing promise that the Spirit of truth, sent from the Father, will testify about Jesus, paired immediately with the disciples' own testimony, "because you have been with me from the beginning" (15:26-27): two complementary forms of witness named side by side rather than as competing claims [239].

THE TEMPLE (JERUSALEM)

Herod the Great's massively enlarged reconstruction of the Second Temple, begun in his eighteenth regnal year (20/19 BCE) and still under active construction during Jesus's own lifetime — the "forty-six years" the crowd cites in John 2:20 [20][21]. Its outer courts, including the space where money-changers and sellers of sacrificial animals conducted business, functioned as a genuine marketplace as well as a sacred precinct; the money-changers existed because the temple's own required half-shekel tax could only be paid in Tyrian silver coinage, which bore the pagan image of the god Melqart-Herakles [19]. The temple John's Jesus disrupts in chapter 2 is the same building the Romans would destroy in 70 CE.

THOMAS (DIDYMUS)

One of the Twelve, called 'Didymus' ('the Twin') at every appearance in this Gospel (11:16; 14:5; 20:24; 21:2), absent from the disciples' locked-room gathering on Easter evening and refusing to believe the others' report without touching Jesus's wounds himself (20:24-25); a week later he is given exactly that opportunity and responds with the confession 'my Lord and my God' (20:28) [330]. The unexplained epithet 'Twin' later generated its own apocryphal traditions [331].

THREEFOLD DENIAL PREDICTION (JOHN 13:36-38)

Jesus's response to Peter's declaration that he is ready to lay down his life for him: a flat prediction that before the rooster crows, Peter will have denied knowing him three times (John 13:38). The same prediction, placed at the Last Supper on the same night, appears independently in all three Synoptic Gospels as well, making its basic placement a point of agreement across the tradition rather than a distinctly Johannine feature [206].

TIBERIAS (CITY)

A city on the western shore of the Sea of Galilee, founded by Herod Antipas, tetrarch of Galilee, around 20 CE and named for the reigning emperor Tiberius. Some boats from Tiberias are noted arriving near the feeding site the following day (6:23); the city's founding within living memory of Jesus's ministry is independently attested by Josephus [90].

TITULUS (THE CROSS INSCRIPTION)

The placard reading `Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews,'' written in Hebrew, Latin, and Greek and fixed above Jesus on the cross (19:19-22), naming the charge for which he was executed. Attaching a titulus naming the condemned's crime was standard, attested Roman execution practice; only John records the chief priests' objection to the wording and Pilate's refusal to change it [304].

TRIUMPHAL ENTRY (JOHN 12:12-19)

Jesus's arrival in Jerusalem the day after the Bethany dinner, greeted by a crowd waving palm branches and shouting `Hosanna,'' mounted on a young donkey in a scene the narrator explicitly ties to Zechariah 9:9 [177]. Some version of a deliberate, symbolically loaded entry into the city just before Passover is widely regarded among historical-Jesus scholars as one of the more historically plausible episodes behind the Gospel accounts, since it helps explain the timing of the authorities' later move against Jesus [179], even though the crowd's scale and unanimity as narrated may reflect later theological amplification [181].

TRUE VINE (JOHN 15 ALLEGORY)

Jesus's declaration "I am the true vine" (15:1), opening an extended allegory in which the Father is the vinedresser, Jesus is the vine, and the disciples are branches that must abide in him to bear fruit. In its original Jewish context this is not a generic pastoral image but a loaded claim to be what Israel itself was called, and repeatedly indicted for failing, to be [224] [225]. The allegory's pruning and burning of unproductive branches (15:2, 6) reflects documented first-century viticulture practice rather than invented imagery [227].

TWO OR THREE WITNESSES (DEUTERONOMY 19:15)

The Torah's forensic principle that no legal matter may be established on a single person's testimony, requiring instead two or three independent witnesses. The discourse of John 5:31-47 is built around this principle, conceding that Jesus's testimony about himself would not meet the legal standard alone and supplying four further witnesses — John the Baptist, Jesus's works, the Father's testimony, and Scripture through Moses — to satisfy it [74] [75] [76] [77].

TWO-LEVEL DRAMA (JOHANNINE REDACTION CRITICISM)

J. Louis Martyn's influential reading method for the Fourth Gospel, which treats episodes like the healing of the man born blind as operating simultaneously on two temporal levels — the level of Jesus's own historical ministry, and the level of the Johannine community's later experience of conflict with and expulsion from synagogue life, projected back into the narrative's dramatic present [125]. Raymond E. Brown's related reconstruction of the Johannine community's own history draws heavily on the same passages [126].

UNITY PRAYER (JOHN 17:21-23, 'THAT THEY MAY ALL BE ONE')

Jesus's prayer that all future believers `may all be one,'' modeled on the Father-Son unity, `so that the world may believe that you have sent me'' (17:21). The passage has an outsized reception history: it is documented as the single most frequently cited New Testament text in twentieth-century Christian ecumenical documents, invoked directly by the World Council of Churches (1948) and Vatican II's Decree on Ecumenism, Unitatis Redintegratio (1964) [266].

VINE AND VINEYARD AS ISRAEL (OLD TESTAMENT METAPHOR TRADITION)

One of the Hebrew Bible's most established images for the nation of Israel, appearing across the Torah, Prophets, and Writings: Psalm 80:8-16's transplanted and ravaged vine, Isaiah 5:1-7's song of the vineyard, Jeremiah 2:21's degenerated wild shoots of a foreign vine, Ezekiel 15 and 19:10-14's vine fit only for burning, and Hosea 10:1's luxuriant vine turned to idolatry [224]. John 15's true vine allegory assumes this whole tradition as background rather than introducing the image from scratch.

VOICE FROM HEAVEN (JOHN 12:28-29)

A voice answering Jesus's prayer to the Father to `glorify your name,'' heard by the crowd standing by as either thunder or an angel speaking (12:28-29). It is the only heavenly voice recorded anywhere in the Fourth Gospel, which narrates neither a baptismal voice (contrast Mark 1:11) nor a Transfiguration scene at all, prompting some commentators to read the episode as a relocated or compressed echo of that wider Synoptic tradition rather than a freestanding Johannine invention [186].

WATER-LIBATION CEREMONY (NISUCH HA-MAYIM)

The daily Sukkot rite in which a priest drew water from the Pool of Siloam and poured it at the Temple altar alongside the wine libation, attested in the Mishnah tractate Sukkah and read by mainstream commentary as the concrete ritual backdrop for Jesus's rivers of living water declaration on the festival's last day (7:37-39) [108].

WOMAN CAUGHT IN ADULTERY

The unnamed woman brought before Jesus in the Temple by scribes and Pharisees who cite the Mosaic penalty of stoning for adultery (8:3-5), and to whom Jesus finally says, after her accusers disperse one by one, "neither do I condemn you; go, and from now on sin no more" (8:11). Like the man born blind and the Samaritan woman at the well elsewhere in John, she is never given a name in the text.

ZECHARIAH (PROPHET)

A postexilic Judean prophet, active c. 520-518 BCE per the book of Zechariah's own superscriptions, whose oracle envisioning a king entering Jerusalem `humble, and mounted on a donkey, on a colt, the foal of a donkey'' (Zechariah 9:9) the narrator explicitly cites as fulfilled by Jesus's entry into Jerusalem on a donkey's colt (12:14-15). John's quotation is a loose paraphrase rather than a verbatim reproduction of either the Hebrew or the Greek Septuagint text [177].
